Or someone, perhaps, would be saying the one supremely important, revealing, apocalyptic thing that she had been waiting all her life to hear. “There!” she could imagine somebody winding up, “that’s the secret of the Universe. What a pity poor Lilian should have gone to bed. She would have loved to hear it.” Good night—it was like parting with a shy lover who had not yet ventured to declare himself. A minute more and he would speak, would reveal himself the unique soul-mate. Good night, and he would remain forever merely diffident little Mr. Jones. Must she part with this day too, before it was transfigured?

===Chinese===
断然地道一声晚安、就此作别，是奥尔德温克尔太太觉得最要命地难做到的事。说出那两个致命的字，她就又对一天宣判了死刑（又一天啊，而如今日子这么少，这么痛苦地短促）；她至少也暂时对自己宣判了死刑。因为那句话一旦最终说出口，她就只剩一件事可做：蹑出灯光之外，把自己埋进睡眠的黑色无意识里。六个钟头、八个钟头将从她身上偷走，再也不会归还。而她直挺挺躺在被单之间装殓自己的时候，谁知道会有多少美妙的事情发生！种种非凡的幸福也许会登门，发现她睡着、听不见它们的召唤，便掉头而去。或者，说不定有谁正要说那句顶顶要紧的、揭底的、启示录式的话——她一辈子等着要听的那句话。“喏！”她能想象有人这样收尾，“宇宙的秘密就是这个。可惜呀，可怜的莉琳已经上床了。」——她要是听着了该多喜欢。”道晚安——就像同一个还没敢吐露心迹的羞怯情人分手。再有一分钟他就要开口了，就要显出自己正是那个独一无二的心灵知音。道了晚安，他就永远只是那个畏畏缩缩的小个子琼斯先生。这一天才刚要变容显圣，难道她也非同它分手不可么？

===Original===
Good night. Every evening she put off the saying of it as long as she possibly could. It was generally half-past one or two before she could bring herself to leave the drawing-room. And even then the words were not finally spoken. For on the threshold of her bedchamber she would halt, desperately renewing the conversation with whichever of her guests had happened to light her upstairs. Who knew? Perhaps in these last five minutes, in the intimacy, in the nocturnal silence, the important thing really would be said. The five minutes often lengthened themselves out to forty, and still Mrs. Aldwinkle stood there, desperately putting off and putting off the moment when she would have to pronounce the sentence of death.

===Chinese===
道晚安。每天晚上，她都要把这句话能拖多久便拖多久。一般总要挨到一点半或两点，她才狠得下心离开客厅。即便到了那时，这句话也不算最终说完。因为一到卧室的门槛上，她就要站住，不管三七二十一地同恰好为她照路上楼的这位或那位客人重新攀谈起来。谁知道呢？也许就在这最后五分钟里，在这份亲密里，在这夜半的寂静里，那件要紧事真的就会说出口。这五分钟常常自己拖延成四十分钟，而奥尔德温克尔太太仍旧站在那里，不管三七二十一地把那个宣判死刑的时刻一拖再拖。

===Original===
When there was nobody else to talk to, she had to be content with the company of Irene, who always, when she herself had undressed, came back in her dressing-gown to help Mrs. Aldwinkle—since it would have been unfair to keep a maid up to such late hours—make ready for the night. Not that little Irene was particularly likely to utter the significant word or think the one apocalyptic thought. Though of course one never knew: out of the mouths of babes and sucklings… And in any case, talking with Irene, who was a dear child and so devoted, was better than definitely condemning oneself to bed.

===Chinese===
没有别人可说话的时候，她只好满足于艾琳作伴；艾琳总是自己宽衣之后，再披着晨衣回来帮奥尔德温克尔太太做就寝的准备——叫女仆陪到那样晚的钟点总是不公平的。倒不是说小艾琳有多大概率说出那句有分量的话、想出那个启示录式的念头。当然，谁也说不准：婴孩和吃奶的口中，也自有睿智……再说，同艾琳说话总好过断然把自己判上床去——那是个可爱的孩子，又那么依依恋人。

===Original===
Tonight, it was one o’clock before Mrs. Aldwinkle made a move towards the door. Miss Thriplow and Mr. Falx, protesting that they too were sleepy, accompanied her. And like an attendant shadow, Irene silently rose when her aunt rose and silently walked after her. Halfway across the room Mrs. Aldwinkle halted and turned round. Formidable she was, a tragedy queen in coral-red velvet. Her little white muslin mirage halted too. Less patient, Mr. Falx and Miss Thriplow moved on towards the door.

===Chinese===
今夜，挨到一点钟，奥尔德温克尔太太才朝门口挪动。思里普洛小姐和法尔克斯先生连声说自己也困了，一路陪着她。艾琳像一道随行的影子，姨妈起身她便无声地起身，无声地跟在后面。走到房间中途，奥尔德温克尔太太站住，回过身来。她是威严逼人的，一位珊瑚红天鹅绒装扮的悲剧女王。她那一袭薄纱白衣的小小蜃景也站住了。法尔克斯先生和思里普洛小姐没这份耐性，径直朝门口走去。

===Original===
“You must all come to bed soon, you know,” she said, addressing herself to the three men who remained at the further end of the room in a tone at once imperious and cajoling. “I simply won’t allow you, Cardan, to keep those poor young men out of their beds to all hours of the night. Poor Calamy has been travelling all day. And Hovenden needs all the sleep, at his age, that he can get.” Mrs. Aldwinkle took it hardly that any of her guests should be awake and talking while she was lying dead in the tomb of sleep. “*Poor* Calamy!” she pathetically exclaimed, as though it were a case of cruelty to animals. She felt herself filled, all at once, with an enormous and maternal solicitude for this young man.

“Yes, poor Calamy!” Mr. Cardan repeated, twinkling. “Out of pure sympathy I was suggesting that we should drink a pint or two of red wine before going to bed. There’s nothing like it for making one sleep.”

===Chinese===
“你们全都得快点上床，记住了，”她说，冲留在房间那头的三个男人发话，口气既专横又哄诱。“卡登，我就是不许你把这两个可怜的年轻人留到深更半夜不睡。可怜的卡拉米赶了一整天路。霍文登呢，在他这个年纪，能睡多少觉就该睡多少觉。”宾客里只要有谁还醒着、还聊着，而她自己却已躺进睡眠的坟墓里装殓，奥尔德温克尔太太便觉得难以忍受。“*可怜的*卡拉米！”她悲天悯人地叫道，仿佛这是桩虐待动物的案子。一时间她觉得自己对这个年轻人满怀博大而母性的关切。

“是啊，可怜的卡拉米！”卡登先生眨着眼应和。“出于纯粹的同情，我刚才正提议，睡前喝上一两品脱红酒。要叫人睡着，没有比它更灵的。”

===Original===
Mrs. Aldwinkle turned her bright blue eyes on Calamy, smiled her sweetest and most piercing smile. “Do come,” she said. “Do.” She extended her hand in a clumsy and inexpressive gesture. “And you, Hovenden,” she added, almost despairingly.

Hovenden looked uncomfortably from Mr. Cardan to Calamy, hoping that one or other of them would answer for him.

“We shan’t be long,” said Calamy. “The time to drink a glass of wine, that’s all. I’m not a bit tired, you know. And Cardan’s suggestion of Chianti is very tempting.”

===Chinese===
奥尔德温克尔太太把那双亮蓝的眼睛转向卡拉米，绽出她最甜美也最扎人的微笑。“一定要来，”她说。“一定。”她伸出手，做了个笨拙而不达意的姿势。“还有你，霍文登，”她几乎是绝望地补了一句。

霍文登不自在地从卡登先生望到卡拉米，巴望着他们哪个替他应一声。

“我们不会久留的，”卡拉来说。“喝杯酒的工夫，仅此而已。您知道，我一点儿也不累。何况卡登提议的基安蒂很诱人。”

===Original===
“Ah well,” said Mrs. Aldwinkle, “if you prefer a glass of wine…” She turned away with a sad indignation and rustled off towards the door, sweeping the tiled floor with the train of her velvet dress. Mr. Falx and Miss Thriplow, who had been lingering impatiently near the door, drew back in order that she might make her exit in full majesty. With a face that looked very gravely out of the little window in her bell of copper hair, Irene followed. The door closed behind them.

Calamy turned to Mr. Cardan. “If I prefer a glass of wine?” he repeated on a note of interrogation. “But prefer it to what? She made it sound as if I had had to make a momentous and eternal choice between her and a pint of Chianti—and had chosen the Chianti. It passes my understanding.”

“Ah, but then you don’t know Lilian as well as I do,” said Mr. Cardan. “And now, let’s go and hunt out that flask and some glasses in the dining-room.”

===Chinese===
“唉，好吧，”奥尔德温克尔太太说，“既然您更想要一杯酒……”她带着悲哀的愤懑转过身去，窸窸窣窣朝门口走去，天鹅绒长裙的裙裾扫过瓷砖地面。一直不耐烦地守在门边的法尔克斯先生和思里普洛小姐退开身子，好让她风风光光、威仪十足地退场。艾琳跟在后面，一张脸从她那头铜钟形红发的钟形小窗里极其庄重地朝外望着。门在他们身后合上了。

卡拉米转向卡登先生。“既然我更想要一杯酒？”他把这话带着疑问的调子学了一遍。“更想要——比起什么来？她说得倒好像我不得不在她同一品脱基安蒂之间做一次重大而永恒的抉择——而我还挑了基安蒂。这超出我的理解力了。”

“啊，可您对莉莲的了解就不如我了，”卡登先生说。“行了，咱们去饭厅把那瓶酒和几只杯子找出来吧。”

===Original===
Halfway up the stairs—they were a grand and solemn flight loping gradually upwards under a slanting tunnel of barrel vaulting—Mrs. Aldwinkle paused. “I always think of them,” she said ecstatically, “going up, coming down. Such a spectacle!”

“Who?” asked Mr. Falx.

“Those grand old people.”

“Oh, the tyrants.”

===Chinese===
上楼上一半——那是一段庄严气派的阶梯，在筒形拱顶的斜隧道里缓缓向上盘升——奥尔德温克尔太太停住了。“我总是想起他们，”她心醉神迷地说，“走上，走下。那样一幅景象！”

“谁？”法尔克斯先生问。

“那些显赫的古人。”

“哦，暴君们。”

===Original===
Mrs. Aldwinkle smiled pityingly. “And the poets, the scholars, the philosophers, the painters, the musicians, the beautiful women. You forget those, Mr. Falx.” She raised her hand, as though summoning their spirits from the abyss. Psychical eyes might have seen a jewelled prince with a nose like an anteater’s slowly descending between obsequious human hedges. Behind him a company of buffoons and little hunchbacked dwarfs, stepping cautiously, sidelong, from stair to stair.…

“I forget nothing,” said Mr. Falx. “But I think tyrants are too high a price to pay.”

===Chinese===
奥尔德温克尔太太怜悯地一笑。“还有诗人、学者、哲学家、画家、音乐家、美人。这些您忘了，法尔克斯先生。”她抬起手，仿佛正把他们的英灵从深渊里唤起。灵视的眼睛兴许真能看见：一位遍体珠光宝气的亲王，长着食蚁兽般的长鼻子，在两行谄媚的人墙之间缓缓而下。他身后跟着一队弄臣和驼背的小矮子，蹑着脚，侧着身，一级楼梯一级楼梯地小心翼翼挪下来。……

“我什么也没忘，”法尔克斯先生说。“可我认为，暴君这个价钱太高，不值。”

===Original===
Mrs. Aldwinkle sighed and resumed her climbing. “What a queer fellow Calamy is, don’t you think?” she said, addressing herself to Miss Thriplow. Mrs. Aldwinkle, who liked discussing other people’s characters and who prided herself on her perspicacity and her psychological intuition, found almost everybody “queer,” even, when she thought it worth while discussing her, little Irene. She liked to think that everyone she knew was tremendously complicated; had strange and improbable motives for his simplest actions, was moved by huge, dark passions; cultivated secret vices; in a word, was larger than life and a good deal more interesting. “What did you think of him, Mary?”

“Very intelligent,” thought Miss Thriplow.

===Chinese===
奥尔德温克尔太太叹了口气，继续往上爬。“卡拉米这人真怪，您说是不是？”她对思里普洛小姐说。奥尔德温克尔太太喜欢议论旁人的性格，又为自己的眼力和心理直觉自豪，在她看来几乎人人都是「怪」的，连小艾琳——当她觉得议论她还值当的时候——也不例外。她乐于相信，她认识的每一个人都复杂得了不得；最简单的举动背后都有离奇而不可思议的动机；都被巨大而阴暗的激情驱动着；都培植着隐秘的恶习；一句话，都比生活本身更宏大，也有意思得多。“你觉得他怎么样，玛丽？”

“很聪明，”思里普洛小姐想。

===Original===
“Oh, of course, of course,” Mrs. Aldwinkle agreed almost impatiently; that wasn’t anything much to talk about. “But one hears odd stories of his amorous tastes, you know.” The party halted at the door of Mrs. Aldwinkle’s room. “Perhaps that was one of the reasons,” she went on mysteriously, “why he went travelling all that time—right away from civilization.…” On such a theme a conversation might surely be almost indefinitely protracted; the moment for uttering the final, fatal good night had not yet come.

===Chinese===
“哦，当然，当然，”奥尔德温克尔太太几乎是急不可耐地表示同意；那算不得什么可谈的。“不过，关于他在情爱上的口味，有些古怪的传闻呢，您知道。”一行人在奥尔德温克尔太太的房门前站住。“也许那正是原因之一，”她神秘兮兮地接着说，“为什么他那一阵子一直在外头旅行——远远躲开文明……”这样的题目，一场谈话几乎肯定可以无限期地拖下去；道出那最后、致命的晚安的时刻，还没有到来。

===Original===
Downstairs in the great saloon the three men were sitting over their red wine. Mr. Cardan had already twice refilled his glass. Calamy was within sight of the bottom of his first tumbler; young Lord Hovenden’s was still more than half full. He was not a very accomplished drinker and was afraid of being sick if he swallowed too much of this young and generous brew.

“Bored, you’re just bored. That’s all it is,” Mr. Cardan was saying. He looked at Calamy over the top of his glass and took another sip, as though to his health. “You haven’t met anyone of late who took your fancy; that’s all. Unless, of course, it’s a case of catarrh in the bile ducts.”

“It’s neither,” said Calamy, smiling.

“Or perhaps it’s the first great climacteric. You don’t happen to be thirty-five, I suppose? Five times seven—a most formidable age. Though not quite so serious as sixty-three. That’s the grand climacteric.” Mr. Cardan shook his head. “Thank the Lord, I got past it without dying, or joining the Church of Rome, or getting married. Thank the Lord; but you?”

“I’m thirty-three,” said Calamy.

“A most harmless time of life. Then it’s just boredom. You’ll meet some little ravishment and all the zest will return.”

Young Lord Hovenden laughed in a very ventriloquial, man-of-the-worldly fashion.

===Chinese===
楼下大厅里，三个男人正围着一瓶红酒坐着。卡登先生已经续过两回杯。卡拉米第一杯已望得见底；年轻的霍文登勋爵那杯还剩一大半。他不是个有造诣的酒客，生怕这种年轻气盛的烈酿喝多了会吐。

“厌倦，你就是厌倦了。就这么回事，”卡登先生正说着。他从杯口上边望着卡拉米，又呷了一口，仿佛是祝他健康。“您近来没遇上一个中意的人；就这么点事。当然喽，除非是胆管里犯了卡他。”

“都不是，”卡拉米微笑着说。

“再不然，就是头一个大关年到了。您该不会碰巧是三十五岁吧？五七三十五——顶顶凶险的年纪。不过还没有六十三那么严重。那才是大关年。”卡登先生摇摇头。“感谢上帝，我熬过了它，没死，没入罗马公教，也没结婚。感谢上帝；可您呢？”

“我三十三，”卡拉米说。

“毫无凶险的年纪。那就只是厌倦。您会遇上什么小小的销魂，全部兴致就都回来了。”

年轻的霍文登勋爵笑起来，那笑法很有点腹语家的、老于世故的派头。

===Original===
Calamy shook his head. “But I don’t really want it to return,” he said. “I don’t want to succumb to any more little ravishments. It’s too stupid; it’s too childish. I used to think that there was something rather admirable and enviable about being an *homme à bonnes fortunes*. Don Juan has an honoured place in literature; it’s thought only natural that a Casanova should complacently boast of his successes. I accepted the current view, and when I was lucky in love—and I’ve always been only too deplorably fortunate—I used to think the more highly of myself.”

“We have all thought the same,” said Mr. Cardan. “The weakness is a pardonable one.”

Lord Hovenden nodded and took a sip of wine to show that he entirely agreed with the last speaker.

===Chinese===
卡拉米摇摇头。“可我实在不想要它回来，”他说。“我不想再折服于任何小小的销魂。太蠢了；太孩子气了。我从前一向认为，做一个 *homme à bonnes fortunes*（走桃花运的男人）颇有几分可敬、可羡。唐璜在文学里占着一个尊贵的席位；卡萨诺瓦得意洋洋地夸耀自己的成功，也被视为天经地义。我接受了流俗之见，于是每逢情场得意——而我这一向走运得叫人叹惋——便越发觉得自己了不起。”

“咱们谁没有这么想过，”卡登先生说。“这是个可以原谅的弱点。”

霍文登勋爵点点头，呷了一口酒，表明自己完全赞成刚才发言的这位。


===Original===
“Pardonable, no doubt,” said Calamy. “But when one comes to think it over, not very reasonable. For, after all, there’s nothing really to be very proud of, there’s nothing very much to boast about. Consider first of all the other heroes who have had the same sort of successes—more notable, very probably, and more numerous than one’s own. Consider them. What do you see? Rows of insolent grooms and pugilists; leather-faced ruffians and disgusting old satyrs; louts with curly hair and no brains, and cunning little pimps like weasels; soft-palmed young epicenes and hairy gladiators—a vast army composed of the most odious specimens of humanity. Is one to be proud of belonging to their numbers?”

===Chinese===
“可以原谅，无疑，”卡拉米说。“可是细想起来，并不很有道理。因为，说到底，这里头实在没有什么真值得骄傲的，没有什么可夸耀的。先想想取得过同类战绩的其他英雄们吧——很可能比您更赫赫，也更多。想想他们。您看见什么？一排排趾高气扬的马夫和拳击手；皮革脸的恶棍和恶心老迈的色鬼；卷头发没脑子的粗坯，黄鼠狼一样奸滑的小拉皮条的；手掌软嫩的脂粉气少年和多毛的角斗士——一支由人类最可憎的标本组成的大军。跻身他们的行列，难道值得骄傲么？”

===Original===
“Why not?” asked Mr. Cardan. “One should always thank God for whatever native talents one possesses. If your talent happens to lie in the direction of higher mathematics, praise God; and if in the direction of seduction, praise God just the same. And thanking God, when one comes to examine the process a little closely, is very much the same as boasting or being proud. I see no harm in boasting a little of one’s Casanovesque capacities. You young men are always so damned intolerant. You won’t allow anyone to go to heaven, or hell, or nowhere, whichever the case may be, by any road except the one you happen to approve of.… You should take a leaf out of the Indians’ book. The Indians calculate that there are eighty-four thousand different types of human beings, each with its own way of getting through life. They probably underestimate.”

Calamy laughed. “I only speak for my type,” he said.

“And Hovenden and I for ours,” said Mr. Cardan. “Don’t we, Hovenden?”

“Oh yes. Yes, of course,” Lord Hovenden answered; and for some reason he blushed.

“Proceed,” said Mr. Cardan, refilling his glass.

===Chinese===
“为什么不呢？”卡登先生问。“人有什么天生才能，都该感谢上帝。您的才能碰巧偏在高等数学，赞美上帝；偏在勾引这一道，也一样赞美上帝。而细细推敲起来，感谢上帝这个举动，同自夸和骄傲本来是一回事。为自己那点卡萨诺瓦式的本领稍稍吹嘘一下，我看没什么害处。你们这些年轻人，总是那么该死地不宽容。谁也不许上天堂，不许下地狱，也不许哪儿都不去——不管案情是哪一种——除非走你们恰好认可的那条路。……你们该学学印度人的样。印度人算出来，人有八万四千种，各有各的过活的路子。这八成还是低估了呢。”

卡拉米笑了。“我只替我这一种人说话，”他说。

“霍文登和我替我们那一种，”卡登先生说。“是不是，霍文登？”

“哦，是。是啊，当然，”霍文登勋爵答道；不知怎么，他脸红了。

“接着说，”卡登先生说着，又斟满一杯。

===Original===
“Well then,” Calamy went on, “belonging to the species I do belong to, I can’t take much satisfaction in these successes. The more so when I consider their nature. For either you’re in love with the woman or you aren’t; either you’re carried away by your inflamed imagination (for, after all, the person you’re really violently in love with is always your own invention and the wildest of fancies) or by your senses and your intellectual curiosity. If you aren’t in love, it’s a mere experiment in applied physiology, with a few psychological investigations thrown in to make it a little more interesting. But if you are, it means that you become enslaved, involved, dependent on another human being in a way that’s positively disgraceful, and the more disgraceful the more there is in you to be enslaved and involved.”

“It wasn’t Browning’s opinion,” said Mr. Cardan.

> “The woman yonder, there’s no use in life
 
 
 But just to obtain her.”

“Browning was a fool,” said Calamy.

But Lord Hovenden was silently of opinion that Browning was quite right. He thought of Irene’s face, looking out of the little window in the copper bell.

“Browning belonged to another species,” Mr. Cardan corrected.

“A foolish species, I insist,” said Calamy.

“Well, to tell the truth,” Mr. Cardan admitted, closing his winking eye a little further, “I secretly agree with you about that. I’m not really as entirely tolerant as I should like to be.”

===Chinese===
“那么好吧，”卡拉米接着说，“既然我属于我确实属于的这一物种，这些成功便给不了我多少满足。何况再一考虑它们的性质。因为，你要么爱那个女人，要么不爱；你要么被你发炎的想象力卷走（因为，说到底，你狂热爱恋着的那个对象，从来是你自己的发明，是天字第一号的奇想），要么被你的感官和你智性上的好奇心卷走。你若不爱，那不过是一场应用生理学的实验，外加几项心理学考察，好叫它有趣一点。你若爱，那就意味着你沦为奴隶、被缠住、依附于另一个人，依附得实在不体面——而且你身上可供奴役、可供纠缠的东西越多，就越不体面。”

“布朗宁可不这么看，”卡登先生说。

> 远处的那个女人，人生在世
> 唯一的用处，就是把她得到。

“布朗宁是个傻瓜，”卡拉米说。

可霍文登勋爵心里默默认为，布朗宁说得再对不过。他想起艾琳的脸，从那口铜钟的小窗里朝外望着的那张脸。

“布朗宁属于另一个物种，”卡登先生更正道。

“一个愚蠢的物种，我坚持认为，”卡拉米说。

“说实话，”卡登先生承认，把那只眨缝眼又眯拢了些，“这一点上我暗自是同意您的。我并不真有自己希望的那样彻底宽容。”

===Original===
Calamy was frowning pensively over his own affairs, and without discussing the greater or less degree of Mr. Cardan’s tolerance he went on. “The question is, at the end of it all: what’s the way out? what’s to be done about it? For it’s obvious, as you say, that the little ravishments will turn up again. And appetite grows with fasting. And philosophy, which knows very well how to deal with past and future temptations, always seems to break down before the present, the immediate ones.”

“Happily,” said Mr. Cardan. “For, when all is said, is there a better indoor sport? Be frank with me; *is* there?”

“Possibly not,” said Calamy, while young Lord Hovenden smiled at Mr. Cardan’s last remark, but unenthusiastically, in a rather painful indecision between amusement and horror. “But the point is, aren’t there better occupations for a man of sense than indoor sports, even the best of indoor sports?”

“No,” said Mr. Cardan, with decision.

“For you, perhaps, there mayn’t be. But it seems to me,” Calamy went on, “that I’m beginning to have had enough of sports, whether indoor or out-of-door. I’d like to find some more serious occupation.”

“But that’s easier said than done.” Mr. Cardan shook his head. “For members of our species it’s precious hard to find any occupation that seems entirely serious. Eh?”

Calamy laughed, rather mournfully. “That’s true,” he said. “But at the same time the sports begin to seem rather an outrage on one’s human dignity. Rather immoral, I would say, if the word weren’t so absurd.”

===Chinese===
卡拉米正愁思着自己的心事，眉头微蹙；对卡登先生宽容程度的高下深浅，他不置一词，径直说了下去。“问题是，到头来：出路在哪里？拿它怎么办？因为很显然，正如您说的，那些小小的销魂还会再来。而胃口是越斋戒越大。哲学对付过去的和未来的诱惑自有一套好本事，可一到眼前的、当场的诱惑跟前，就总吃败仗。”

“幸亏如此，”卡登先生说。“因为，说到底，还有比这更好的室内运动么？对我坦白；*有*么？”

“大概没有，”卡拉米说；年轻的霍文登勋爵对卡登先生最后这句俏皮话笑了笑，却笑得不起劲，在觉得有趣和觉得骇然之间痛苦地举棋不定。“可问题在于，一个明白人，难道没有比室内运动——哪怕是最好的室内运动——更好的营生么？”

“没有，”卡登先生决然说。

“对您来说，也许没有。可依我看，”卡拉米接着说，“我对运动，无论室内还是户外，都开始腻了。我想找一件更严肃的营生。”

“说来容易做来难。”卡登先生摇摇头。“咱们这个物种的成员，要找一件显得完全严肃的营生，可太难了。呃？”

卡拉米笑了，笑得颇凄然。“这话不假，”他说。“可与此同时，这些运动开始显得是对人的人格尊严的冒犯了。颇不道德，我要说——倘若『道德』这个词不是这么荒唐的话。”

===Original===
“Not at all absurd, I assure you, when used as you use it.” Mr. Cardan twinkled more and more genially over the top of his glass. “As long as you don’t talk about moral laws and all that sort of thing there’s no absurdity. For, it’s obvious, there are no moral laws. There are social customs on the one hand, and there are individuals with their individual feelings and moral reactions on the other. What’s immoral in one man may not matter in another. Almost nothing, for example, is immoral for me. Positively, you know, I can do anything and yet remain respectable in my own eyes, and in the eyes of others not merely wonderfully decent, but even noble.

> Ah, what avail the loaded dice?
 
 
 Ah, what the tubs of wine?
 
 
 What every weakness, every vice?
 
 
 Tom Cardan, all were thine.

I won’t bore you with the rest of this epitaph which I composed for myself some little time ago. Suffice to say that I point out in the two subsequent stanzas that these things availed absolutely nothing and that, *malgré tout*, I remained the honest, sober, pure and high-minded man that everyone always instinctively recognizes me to be.” Mr. Cardan emptied his glass and reached out once more for the fiasco.

===Chinese===
“一点也不荒唐，我向您保证，只要您照您这么用法去用它。”卡登先生从杯口上边越来越和气地眨着眼。“只要您不谈什么道德律和那一套劳什子，就没有荒唐可言。因为，很显然，压根儿没有道德律这种东西。一边是社会的习俗，另一边是一个个的人，各有各的情感和道德反应。在这个人身上不道德的事，在那个人身上也许无关紧要。比如说，几乎没有什么事对我是罪恶的。千真万确，您知道，我干什么都使得，而在我自己眼里依然体面，在别人眼里不但好得惊人，简直高贵。

> 啊，灌了铅的骰子又有何用？
> 啊，一桶桶的酒又有何益？
> 无论哪种软弱，哪种恶行？
> 汤姆·卡登，样样都归你。

这墓志铭是我前些时给自己做的，余下的部分就不拿它烦你们了。只消说一句：在后面两节里我指出了，这些东西统统毫无用处，而我 *malgré tout*（尽管如此）依旧是那个诚实、清醒、纯洁、高尚的人——人人凭本能一眼就认得出的那个人。”卡登先生喝干杯中酒，又伸手去够那只酒瓶。

===Original===
“You’re fortunate,” said Calamy. “It’s not all of us whose personalities have such a natural odour of sanctity that they can disinfect our septic actions and render them morally harmless. When I do something stupid or dirty I can’t help feeling that it is stupid or dirty. My soul lacks virtues to make it wise or clean. And I can’t dissociate myself from what I do. I wish I could. One does such a devilish number of stupid things. Things one doesn’t want to do. If only one could be a hedonist and only do what was pleasant! But to be a hedonist one must be wholly rational; there’s no such thing as a genuine hedonist, there never has been. Instead of doing what one wants to do or what would give one pleasure, one drifts through existence doing exactly the opposite, most of the time—doing what one has no desire to do, following insane promptings that lead one, fully conscious, into every sort of discomfort, misery, boredom and remorse. Sometimes,” Calamy went on, sighing, “I positively regret the time I spent in the army during the war. Then, at any rate, there was no question of doing what one liked; there was no liberty, no choice. One did what one was told and that was all. Now I’m free; I have every opportunity for doing exactly what I like—and I consistently do what I don’t like.”

“But do you know exactly what you do like?” asked Mr. Cardan.

Calamy shrugged his shoulders. “Not exactly,” he said. “I suppose I should say reading, and satisfying my curiosity about things, and thinking. But about what, I don’t feel perfectly certain. I don’t like running after women, I don’t like wasting my time in futile social intercourse, or in the pursuit of what is technically known as pleasure. And yet for some reason and quite against my will I find myself passing the greater part of my time immersed in precisely these occupations. It’s an obscure kind of insanity.”

===Chinese===
“您真有福，”卡拉米说。“不是每个人的品性都天然带着一股圣洁的气味，能给我们的化脓的行径消消毒、叫它们在道德上无害。我做了蠢事或脏事，就忍不住觉得它蠢、觉得它脏。我的灵魂缺少使它聪明、使它干净的美德。而我没办法同我做的事一刀两断。但愿能够。人做的蠢事多得吓人。净是些不想做的事。人要是能做个享乐主义者，只做愉快的事，该多好！可要做享乐主义者，就得全然理性；世上没有真正的享乐主义者，从来没有过。人不去做想做的事、不去做会带来快乐的事，反而在生存里随波逐流，大半时间做的恰恰相反——做毫无兴致做的事，追随疯狂的驱策，心知肚明地走进各式各样的不适、悲惨、无聊和悔恨。”卡拉米叹了口气，接着说，“有时我当真惋惜起战争年代在军队里度过的那段时光。那时候至少谈不上做自己喜欢的事；没有自由，没有选择。叫你做什么你就做什么，仅此而已。如今我自由了；做什么喜欢的，机会要多少有多少——而我偏偏一以贯之地做我不喜欢的。”

“可您确切地知道自己喜欢什么吗？”卡登先生问。

卡拉米耸耸肩。“不确切，”他说。“我想我该说是读书，是满足自己对万事万物的好奇，是思考。可思考什么，我没有十足把握。我不喜欢追女人，不喜欢把时间浪费在无益的社交应酬上，浪费在追求那种术语上所谓『快乐』的东西上。可不知为什么，又完全违背我的意愿，我发现自己把大部分时间恰恰泡在这些营生里。这是一种说不清道不明的疯狂。”

===Original===
Young Lord Hovenden, who knew that he liked dancing and desired Irene Aldwinkle more than anything in the world, found all this a little incomprehensible. “I can’t see what vere is to prevent a man from doing what he wants to do. Except,” he qualified, remembering the teaching of Mr. Falx, “economic necessity.”

“And himself,” added Mr. Cardan.

“And what’s the most depressing of all,” Calamy went on, without paying attention to the interruption, “is the feeling that one will go on like this forever, in the teeth of every effort to stop. I sometimes wish I weren’t externally free. For then at any rate I should have something to curse at, for getting in my way, other than my own self. Yes, positively, I sometimes wish I were a navvy.”

“You wouldn’t if you had ever been one,” said Lord Hovenden, gravely and with a knowing air of speaking from personal experience.

Calamy laughed. “You’re perfectly right,” he said, and drained his glass. “Shouldn’t we think of going to bed?”

===Chinese===
年轻的霍文登勋爵——他知道自己喜欢跳舞，天下最想要的就是艾琳·奥尔德温克尔——觉得这一切有点费解。“我看不出素什么能拦住一个人，叫人做不成自己想做的事。除素，”他想起法尔克斯先生的教导，补上一条限定，“经济上的必需。”

“还有他自己，”卡登先生补充道。

“而最叫人丧气的，”卡拉米不理会这插话，接着说，“是那种感觉：人将永远这样下去，怎么下力气都拦不住。有时我希望自己并非身在外在的自由里。因为那样我至少还有个可咒骂的物件，怪它挡我的路，而不必是我自己。真的，千真万确，有时我希望自己是个挖沟的苦力。”

“您要是真当过，就不会这么想了，”霍文登勋爵说，语气庄重，一副深知就里、仿佛亲身经历过的神气。

卡拉米笑了。“您说得再对不过，”他说，把杯中残酒喝干。“咱们是不是该想着上床了？”

### VIII / VIII

===Original===
To Irene fell the privilege every evening of brushing her aunt’s hair. For her these midnight moments were the most precious in the day. True, it was sometimes an agony for her to keep awake and the suppression of yawns was always painful; three years of incessant practice had not yet accustomed her to her Aunt Lilian’s late hours. Aunt Lilian used to twit her sometimes on her childish longing for sleep; at other times she used to insist, very solicitously, that Irene should rest after lunch and go to bed at ten. The teasing made Irene feel ashamed of her babyishness; the solicitude made her protest that she wasn’t a baby, that she was never tired and could easily do with five or six hours’ sleep a night. The important thing, she had found, was not to be seen yawning by Aunt Lilian and always to look fresh and lively. If Aunt Lilian noticed nothing there was neither teasing nor solicitude.

===Chinese===
每天晚上替姨妈刷头的特权落在艾琳身上。对她来说，这午夜时分是一天里最可宝贵的。当然，有时候保持醒着对她是种苦刑，忍住呵欠也总是难受；可三年不间断的操练，仍旧没让她习惯莉莲姨妈的晚钟点。莉莲姨妈有时要打趣她孩子气地贪睡；有时又极其体贴地坚持，艾琳午饭后该歇一歇，十点就该上床。打趣让艾琳为自己的幼稚羞惭；体贴又让她申辩说自己不是娃娃，说自己从来不累，一夜睡五六个钟头绰绰有余。她已摸清了要领：要紧的是别让莉莲姨妈撞见自己打呵欠，永远显得新鲜活泼。莉莲姨妈什么也没察觉，就既没有打趣，也没有体贴。

===Original===
But in any case, every inconvenience was paid for a thousand times by the delights of these confidential conversations in front of the dressing-table mirror. While the young girl brushed and brushed away at the long tresses of pale golden-brown hair, Mrs. Aldwinkle, her eyes shut, and with an expression of beatitude on her face—for she took a cat’s pleasure in the brushing—would talk, spasmodically, in broken sentences, of the events of the day, of her guests, of the people they had met; or of her own past, of plans for the future—hers or Irene’s—of love. On all these subjects Mrs. Aldwinkle spoke intimately, confidentially, without reserve. Feeling that she was being treated by her Aunt Lilian as entirely grownup and almost as an equal, Irene was proud and grateful. Without deliberately setting out to complete the subjugation of her niece, Mrs. Aldwinkle had discovered, in those midnight conversations, the most perfect means for achieving this end. If she talked like this to Irene, it was merely because she felt the need of talking intimately to someone, and because there was nobody else to talk to. Incidentally, however, she had contrived in the process to make the girl her slave. Made her Aunt Lilian’s confidante, invested, so to speak, with a title of honour, Irene felt a gratitude which strengthened her original childish attachment to her aunt.

===Chinese===
但无论如何，梳妆镜前这些闺中密谈的欢愉，把一切不便都千倍万倍地偿还了。少女一下一下刷着那头淡金褐色的长发，奥尔德温克尔太太阖着眼，一脸至福的表情——刷头之于她，是猫一般的享受——断断续续、零零碎碎地说着话：说当天的事，说她的宾客，说他们遇见的人；或者说她自己的过去，说将来的打算——她的或艾琳的——说爱情。所有这些题目，奥尔德温克尔太太都说得亲密、知心、毫无保留。感到莉莲姨妈把自己完全当大人、几乎当平辈看待，艾琳又骄傲又感激。奥尔德温克尔太太并没有存心要完成对侄女的彻底降服，却在那些午夜谈话里发现了达到这一目的的最完美的手段。她之所以对艾琳这样讲，无非因为她需要有人亲密地说说话，而又没有别人可说。然而附带地，她就在这过程中设法叫这姑娘做了她的奴隶。被立为莉莲姨妈的心腹，可以说得了个荣誉头衔，艾琳感到的感激，又给她原先孩子气的依恋添了力道。

===Original===
Meanwhile, she had learned to talk with an airy familiarity of many things concerning which young girls are supposed to be ignorant, and of which, indeed, she herself knew, except intellectually and at second hand, nothing. She had learned to be knowing and worldly wise, in the void, so to speak, and with no personal knowledge of the world. Gravely, ingenuously, she would say things that could only be uttered out of the depths of the profoundest innocence, amplifying and making embarrassingly explicit in public things that Mrs. Aldwinkle had only fragmentarily hinted at in the confidential small hours. She regarded herself as immensely mature.

===Chinese===
与此同时，她还学会了用一种轻飘飘的熟络口气谈论许多事情——按理说少女们对那些事一无所知，而实际上，她自己也的确一无所知，除了道听途说的一点书本知识。她学会了无所不知、老于世故——可以说是在真空里学会的，对世界毫无亲身的认识。她会一本正经、天真烂漫地说出一些只有在最深最深的无知深处才说得出口的话，把奥尔德温克尔太太在交心的深夜里只零零碎碎暗示过的事情，当众引申发挥，说得叫人难堪。她自认成熟得了不得。

===Original===
Tonight Mrs. Aldwinkle was in a rather gloomy, complaining mood.

“I’m getting old,” she said, sighing, and opening her eyes for a moment to look at her image in the glass that confronted her. The image did not deny the statement. “And yet I always feel so young.”

===Chinese===
今夜奥尔德温克尔太太的心绪相当阴郁，怨气冲冲。

“我老了，”她叹着气说，睁眼朝镜子里自己的映像看了一眼。那映像并不否认这句话。“可我一直觉得自己这么年轻。”

===Original===
“That’s what really matters,” Irene declared. “And besides, it’s nonsense; you’re not old; you don’t look old.” In Irene’s eyes, moreover, she really didn’t look old.

“People don’t like one any more when one gets old,” Mrs. Aldwinkle continued. “Friends are terribly faithless. They fall away.” She sighed. “When I think of all the friends…” She left the sentence unfinished.

===Chinese===
“要紧的正是这个，”艾琳断言。“再说，这是胡说；您不老；您看上去也不老。”何况在艾琳眼里，她看上去确实不老。

“人一老，人家就不喜欢你了，”奥尔德温克尔太太接着说。“朋友们薄情得很。一个个都散了。”她叹了口气。“我一想起那些朋友……”话到半截，她没说下去。

===Original===
All her life long Mrs. Aldwinkle had had a peculiar genius for breaking with her friends and lovers. Mr. Cardan was almost the sole survivor from an earlier generation of friends. From all the rest she had parted, and she had parted with a light heart. It had seemed easy to her, when she was younger, to make new friends in place of the old. Potential friends, she thought, were to be found everywhere, every day. But now she was beginning to doubt whether the supply was, after all, so inexhaustible as she had once supposed. People of her own age, she found, were already set fast in the little social worlds they had made for themselves. And people of the younger generation seemed to find it hard to believe that she felt, in her heart, just as young as they did. They mostly treated her with the rather distant politeness which one accords to a stranger and an elder person.

===Chinese===
奥尔德温克尔太太平生有一种特殊的天才：同朋友和情人绝交。卡登先生几乎是上一辈朋友里仅存的幸存者。其余的她都一一分手了，而且分手分得轻松愉快。年轻时她觉得，另结新交以代替旧友，易如反掌。潜在的朋友，她想，到处每天都有。可如今她开始怀疑，这货源毕竟不像她当初设想的那么取之不尽。她发现，跟她同辈的人早已牢牢凝固在各自营造的小社交天地里。而年轻一辈的人，似乎又很难相信她心里觉得自己同他们一样年轻。他们对她多半是那种敬而远之的客气——人们对陌生人和长辈用的那种。

===Original===
“I think people are horrid,” said Irene, giving a particularly violent sweep with the hairbrush to emphasize her indignation.

“You won’t be faithless?” asked Mrs. Aldwinkle.

Irene bent over and, for all answer, kissed her on the forehead. Mrs. Aldwinkle opened her glittering blue eyes and looked up at her, smiling, as she did so, that siren smile that, for Irene, was still as fascinating as it had ever been.

“If only everybody were like my little Irene!” Mrs. Aldwinkle let her head fall forward and once more closed her eyes. There was a silence. “What are you sighing about in that heartbreaking way?” she suddenly asked.

===Chinese===
“我看这些人可恶，”艾琳说，手里那把发刷格外用力地扫了一下，好强调她的义愤。

“你不会薄情吧？”奥尔德温克尔太太问。

艾琳俯下身去，算作全部回答，在她额头上吻了一下。奥尔德温克尔太太睁开那双亮闪闪的蓝眼睛，仰望着她，同时绽出那个海妖式的微笑——在艾琳眼里，它至今魅力不减当年。

“要是人人都像我的小艾琳就好了！”奥尔德温克尔太太让头往前一垂，重新阖上眼。一阵沉默。“你叹那么令人心碎的气做什么？”她突然问。

===Original===
Irene’s blush ran tingling up into her temples and disappeared under the copper-coloured fringe. “Oh, nothing,” she said, with an off-handedness that expressed the depth of her guilty embarrassment. That deep intake of breath, that brief and passionate expiry were not the components of a sigh. She had been yawning with her mouth shut.

But Mrs. Aldwinkle, with her bias towards the romantic, did not suspect the truth. “Nothing, indeed!” she echoed incredulously. “Why, it was the noise of the wind blowing through the cracks of a broken heart. I never heard such a sigh.” She looked at the reflection of Irene’s face in the mirror. “And you’re blushing like a peony. What is it?”

===Chinese===
艾琳的红晕麻酥酥地爬上太阳穴，消失在那圈铜色的刘海底下。“哦，没什么，”她说，那份漫不经心正泄露了她愧疚窘迫之深。那深深的一口气，那短促而热烈的一呼，并不是叹息的组成部分。她是闭着嘴在打呵欠。

可奥尔德温克尔太太偏向浪漫一路，压根儿没想到真相。“没什么，真是！”她不置信地学了一遍。“嘿，那分明是风吹过一颗破碎的心上的裂缝的声音。我从没听见过这样的叹息。”她望着镜子里艾琳的脸。“你还脸红得像朵芍药。到底是怎么回事？”

===Original===
“But it’s nothing, I tell you,” Irene declared, speaking almost in a tone of irritation. She was annoyed with herself for having yawned so ineptly and blushed so pointlessly, rather than with her aunt. She immersed herself more than ever deeply in her brushing, hoping and praying that Mrs. Aldwinkle would drop the subject.

But Mrs. Aldwinkle was implacable in her tactlessness. “I never heard anything that sounded so lovesick,” she said, smiling archly into the looking-glass. Mrs. Aldwinkle’s humorous sallies had a way of falling ponderously, like bludgeon strokes, on the objects of her raillery. One never knew, when she was being sprightly, whether to feel sorrier for the victim or for Mrs. Aldwinkle herself. For though the victim might get hard knocks, the spectacle of Mrs. Aldwinkle laboriously exerting herself to deliver them was sadly ludicrous; one wished, for her sake, for the sake of the whole human race, that she would desist. But she never did. Mrs. Aldwinkle always carried all her jokes to the foreseen end, and generally far further than was foreseeable by anyone less ponderously minded than herself. “It was like a whale sighing!” she went on with a frightful playfulness. “It must be a grand passion of the largest size. Who is it? Who is it?” She raised her eyebrows, she smiled with what seemed to her, as she studied it in the glass, a most wickedly sly but charming smile—like a smile in a comedy by Congreve, it occurred to her.

===Chinese===
“可没什么，我跟您说，”艾琳几乎带着恼怒的口气申明。她恼的是自己呵欠打得那么不是时候、脸红得那么没来由，倒不是恼姨妈。她越发深深地埋头刷起头来，一面盼着、一面祷告奥尔德温克尔太太换个题目。

可奥尔德温克尔太太的不知趣是不依不饶的。“我从没听见过这么害相思的声音，”她说，对着镜子狡黠地笑。奥尔德温克尔太太的诙谐出击，总有那么一股子沉甸甸的劲头，像抡大棒似的落在她取笑的对象身上。每逢她活泼俏皮起来，你永远不知道该更可怜那个挨打的，还是该更可怜奥尔德温克尔太太本人。因为挨打的固然要吃几下重的，可奥尔德温克尔太太费尽九牛二虎之力去抡那几棒的样子，实在可悲又可笑；你为了她、为了全人类的体面，都巴望她住手。可她从来不住。奥尔德温克尔太太总是把她所有的笑话都执行到预见的终点，而且一般还远远超出——超出任何脑子没她那么沉的人所能预见的程度。“那像一头鲸鱼在叹气！”她带着可怕的顽皮劲儿接着说。“这准是一场顶大规格的轰轰烈烈的恋爱。是谁？是谁？”她扬起眉毛，绽出一个笑容——她在镜子里端详着，觉得那笑真是狡黠得邪恶，却又迷人——她忽然想到，像康格里夫喜剧里的一个微笑。

===Original===
“But, Aunt Lilian,” protested Irene, almost in despair, almost in tears, “it was nothing, I tell you.” At moments like this she could almost find it in her to hate Aunt Lilian. “As a matter of fact, I was only…” She was going to blurt it out courageously; she was just going to tell Aunt Lilian—at the risk of a teasing or an almost equally unwelcome solicitude: either were better than this—that she had been merely yawning. But Mrs. Aldwinkle, still relentlessly pursuing her fun, interrupted her.

“But I guess who it is,” she said, wagging a forefinger at the glass. “I guess. I’m not such a blind stupid old auntie as you think. You imagine I haven’t noticed. Silly child! Did she think I didn’t see that he was very assiduous and that she rather liked it? Did she think her stupid old auntie was blind?”

===Chinese===
“可是，莉莲姨妈，”艾琳几乎绝望、几乎带着哭腔地抗议，“没什么，我跟您说。”这种时候她几乎能恨起莉莲姨妈来。“其实，我只是……”她正要鼓起勇气一吐为快；她正要告诉莉莲姨妈——豁出去挨一场打趣，或者一场同样不受欢迎的体贴：两样都比眼下这样强——说她不过是打了个呵欠。可奥尔德温克尔太太还穷追着她的玩笑不放，打断了她。

“可我猜到是谁了，”她朝镜子摇着一根食指。“我猜到了。我可不是你想的那种又瞎又蠢的老姨妈。你当我没留意。傻孩子！她以为我没看见他多么殷勤、她又相当受用么？她以为她这蠢老姨妈是瞎子么？”

===Original===
Irene blushed again; the tears came into her eyes. “But who are you talking about?” she said in a voice that she had to make a great effort to keep from breaking and trembling out of control.

“What an innocent!” mocked Mrs. Aldwinkle, still very Congreve. And at this point—earlier than was usual with her on these occasions—she had mercy and consented to put poor Irene out of her agony. “Why, Hovenden,” she said. “Who else should it be?”

“Hovenden?” Irene repeated with genuine surprise.

“Injured innocence!” Mrs. Aldwinkle momentarily renewed her trampling fun. “But it’s sufficiently obvious,” she went on in a more natural voice. “The poor boy follows you like a dog.”

===Chinese===
艾琳又红了脸；眼泪涌上眼眶。“可您说的是谁呀？”她说，那嗓音她得费好大的劲才守得住，没碎掉、没抖得失了控。

“真是个天真无邪的！”奥尔德温克尔太太嘲道，还是十足康格里夫的做派。而到这里——比她平日这种场合收手得早——她发了慈悲，答应把可怜的艾琳从煎熬里解出来。“嗐，霍文登呗，”她说。“还能是谁？”

“霍文登？”艾琳重复了一遍，当真吃了一惊。

“无辜受屈了！”奥尔德温克尔太太一时间又续上她那踩踏式的玩笑。“可这不是明摆着的么，”她换了个自然些的嗓音接着说。“那可怜的孩子跟条狗似的跟着你。”

===Original===
“Me?” Irene had been too much preoccupied in following her Aunt Lilian to notice that she in her turn was being followed.

“Now don’t pretend,” said Mrs. Aldwinkle. “It’s so stupid pretending. Much better to be frank and straightforward. Admit, now, that you like him.”

Irene admitted. “Yes, of course I like him. But not… not in any special way. I’d really not thought of him like that.”

===Chinese===
“我？”艾琳一心只顾着追随莉莲姨妈，没留意自己身后也有人追随。

“得了，别装了，”奥尔德温克尔太太说。“装模作样多蠢。坦率直爽多好。现在承认吧，你喜欢他。”

艾琳承认了。“是，我当然喜欢他。可不是……不是那种特别的喜欢。我真的没往那方面想过他。”

===Original===
A shade contemptuously, benevolently amused, Mrs. Aldwinkle smiled. She forgot her depression, forgot her causes of personal complaint against the universal order of things. Absorbed in the uniquely interesting subject, in the sole and proper study of mankind, she was once more happy. Love—it was the only thing. Even Art, compared with it, hardly existed. Mrs. Aldwinkle was almost as much interested in other people’s love as in her own. She wanted everyone to love, constantly and complicatedly. She liked to bring people together, to foster tender feelings, to watch the development of passion, to assist—when it happened; and Mrs. Aldwinkle was always rather disappointed when it did not—at the tragic catastrophe. And then, when the first love, growing old, had lingeringly or violently died, there was the new love to think of, to arrange, to foster, to watch; and then the third, the fourth.… One must always follow the spontaneous motions of the heart; it is the divine within us that stirs in the heart. And one must worship Eros so reverently that one can never be content with anything but the most poignant, most passionate manifestations of his power. To be content with a love that has turned in the course of time to mere affection, kindliness and quiet comprehension is almost to blaspheme against the name of Eros. Your true lover, thought Mrs. Aldwinkle, leaves the old, paralytic love and turns wholeheartedly to the young passion.

===Chinese===
奥尔德温克尔太太微微含着点轻蔑、又好心地觉得有趣地笑了。她忘了自己的消沉，忘了自己对天地万般秩序的那一肚子私人怨气。她沉浸在这个独一无二有趣味的题目里，沉浸在人世间唯一正当的研究对象里，重新快乐起来。爱情——只有这才是正经事。就连艺术同它一比，也几乎算不得存在。奥尔德温克尔太太对旁人恋爱的兴趣，几乎不亚于对她自己的。她希望人人都在恋爱，恋得经常，恋得曲折。她喜欢撮合人，培植柔情，旁观激情的发展，到场协助——等它发生的时刻；而不发生的时候，奥尔德温克尔太太总要相当失望——那场悲剧性的大难。然后，等初恋年事渐高，拖沓地或暴烈地死去，又有新的恋爱可想、可安排、可培植、可旁观；然后是第三场，第四场。……人必须永远追随心灵的自发冲动；在我们心里鼓动的，正是我们心中的神性。人必须崇拜厄洛斯，崇拜得如此虔敬，以至于除了他威力最惨烈、最炽烈的显形，再不能满足于任何别的。满足于一场随岁月流转沦为单纯情谊、善意与安静理解的爱，就近乎渎犯厄洛斯的名。真正的情人，奥尔德温克尔太太想，会撇下那瘫痪的旧爱，一心一意转向年轻的激情。

===Original===
“What a goose you are!” said Mrs. Aldwinkle. “I sometimes wonder,” she went on, “whether you’re capable of being in love at all, you’re so uncomprehending, so cold.”

Irene protested with all the energy of which she was capable. One could not have lived as long as she had in Mrs. Aldwinkle’s company without regarding the imputation of coldness, of insensitiveness to passion, as the most damning of all possible impeachments. It was better to be accused of being a murderess—particularly if it were a case of *crime passionnel*. “I don’t know how you say that,” she said indignantly. “I’m always in love.” Had there not been Peter, and Jacques, and Mario?

===Chinese===
“你这只呆头鹅！”奥尔德温克尔太太说。“我有时纳闷，”她接着说，“你到底会不会恋爱，你怎么这么不开窍，这么冷。”

艾琳拿出全身的力气抗议。在奥尔德温克尔太太身边活了这么久，不可能不把冷淡的指摘、对激情无动于衷的指摘，看作一切指控里最致命的一条。倒不如被指控做杀人犯——尤其是那种*情罪*案。“真不知道您怎么说得出口，”她愤愤地说。“我总是在恋爱。”不是有过彼得，有过雅克，还有过马里奥么？

===Original===
“You may think you have,” said Mrs. Aldwinkle contemptuously, forgetting that it was she herself who had persuaded Irene that she was in love. “But it was more imagination than the real thing. Some women are born like that.” She shook her head. “And they die like that.” One might have inferred from Mrs. Aldwinkle’s words and the tone of her voice that Irene was a superannuated spinster of forty, proved conclusively, after twenty years of accumulated evidence, to be incapable of anything remotely resembling an amorous passion.

Irene made no answer, but went on brushing her aunt’s hair. Mrs. Aldwinkle’s aspersions were particularly wounding to her. She wished that she could do something startling to prove their baselessness. Something spectacular.

“And I’ve always thought Hovenden an extremely nice boy,” Mrs. Aldwinkle continued, with the air of pursuing an argument. She talked on. Irene listened and went on brushing.

===Chinese===
“你自己以为是罢了，”奥尔德温克尔太太轻蔑地说，忘了当初说服艾琳相信自己在恋爱的，正是她本人。“可那多半是想象，不是真格的。有些女人生来就那样。”她摇摇头。“到死也那样。”从奥尔德温克尔太太的话音里，人家简直可以推断：艾琳是个过气的四十岁老处女，二十年间证据累积如山，铁案如山地证明她连勉强近似恋爱的东西也无能为之。

艾琳不答话，只顾继续刷姨妈的头。奥尔德温克尔太太这番贬抑特别伤她。她但愿能干出一件惊人的事，证明这些话纯属无稽。一件轰轰烈烈的事。

“再说，我一向觉得霍文登是个好得不得了的孩子，”奥尔德温克尔太太接着说，一副把论证推进到底的架势。她讲个不停。艾琳听着，手底下刷个不停。

### IX / IX

===Original===
In the silence and solitude of her room, Miss Thriplow sat up for a long time, pen in hand, in front of an open notebook. “Darling Jim,” she wrote, “darling Jim. Today you came back to me so suddenly and unexpectedly that I could almost have cried aloud in front of all those people. Was it an accident that I picked that stiff leaf from Apollo’s tree and crushed it to fragrance between my fingers? Or were you there? was it you who secretly whispered to the unconscious part of me, telling me to pick that leaf? I wonder; oh, I wonder and wonder. Sometimes I believe that there are no accidents, that we do nothing by chance. Tonight I felt sure of it.

===Chinese===
在她房间的一片寂静与独处中，思里普洛小姐久久地坐着，手里握着笔，面前摊开一本笔记簿。「亲爱的吉姆，」她写道，「亲爱的吉姆。今天你这样突然、这样出乎意料地回到我身边，我险些当着那些人的面哭出声来。我从阿波罗的树上摘下那片硬挺的叶子、用手指把它碾出香气来，这是偶然么？还是你在那里？是你在悄悄对我身上无意识的那部分耳语，叫我摘那片叶子么？我纳闷；哦，我纳闷了又纳闷。有时我相信世上没有偶然，我们做什么都不是碰巧。今夜我确信了。

===Original===
“But I wonder what made you want to remind me of Mr. Chigwell’s little shop at Weltringham. Why did you want to make me see you sitting in the barber’s chair, so stiff and grownup, with the wheel of the mechanical brush still turning overhead and Mr. Chigwell saying, ‘Hair’s very dry, Mr. Thriplow’? And the rubber driving band used always to remind me…” Miss Thriplow recorded the simile of the wounded snake which had first occurred to her this evening. There was no particular reason why she should have antedated the conceit and attributed its invention to her childhood. It was just a question of literary tact; it seemed more interesting if one said that it had been made up when one was a child; that was all. “I ask myself whether there is any particular significance in this reminder. Or perhaps it’s just that you find me neglectful and unremembering—poor darling, darling Jim—and take whatever opportunity offers of reminding me that you existed, that you still exist. Forgive me, Jim. Everybody forgets. We should all be kind and good and unselfish if we always remembered—remembered that other people are just as much alive and individual and complicated as we are, remembered that everybody can be just as easily hurt, that everybody needs love just as much, that the only visible reason why we exist in the world is to love and be loved. But that’s no excuse for me. It’s no excuse for anyone to say that other people are just as bad. I ought to remember more. I oughtn’t to let my mind be choked with weeds. It’s not only the memory of you that the weeds choke; it’s everything that’s best and most delicate and finest. Perhaps you reminded me of Mr. Chigwell and the bay rum in order to remind me at the same time to love more, and admire more, and sympathize more, and be more aware. Darling Jim.”

===Chinese===
「可我纳闷，你为什么偏要让我想起奇格韦尔先生在韦尔特林厄姆的那家小店。你为什么要让我看见你坐在理发椅里，那么僵硬、那么大人气，头顶上机械刷的转轮还在转，奇格韦尔先生说着：『头发很干枯啊，思里普洛先生』？还有那条橡胶传动带，从前总让我想起……」思里普洛小姐把那条受伤的蛇的比喻记录了下来——就是今晚头一回冒出来的那个。她把这个妙想往前提、说成童年所创，并没有什么特别的理由。这不过是文学上的取巧；一件事若说成孩提时想出来的，就显得更有趣味；如此而已。「我问自己，这番提醒可有什么特别的深意。也许不过是你嫌我怠慢、健忘——可怜的亲爱的、亲爱的吉姆——于是逮着什么机会便提醒我：你存在过，你如今仍然存在。原谅我，吉姆。人人都会忘。倘若我们永远记得——记得别人同我们一样是活生生、有个性、复杂的人，记得谁都一样容易受伤，谁都一样需要爱，记得我们活在世上唯一看得见的理由就是爱与被爱——我们就全都会善良、仁慈、无私。可这不能做我的借口。谁也不能拿『别人也一样坏』来作借口。我应当记得更多。我不该任凭我的心里长满杂草。杂草窒息的不只是对你的记忆；还有一切最好的、最精微、最精致的东西。也许你让我想起奇格韦尔先生和月桂朗姆水，正是为了同时提醒我：要爱得更多，钦佩得更多，体恤得更多，觉知得更多。亲爱的吉姆。」

===Original===
She put down her pen, and looking out through the open window at the starry sky she tried to think of him, tried to think of death. But it was difficult to think of death. It was difficult, she found, to keep the mind uninterruptedly on the idea of extinction, of non-life instead of life, of nothingness. In books one reads about sages meditating. She herself had often tried to meditate. But somehow it never seemed to come to much. All sorts of little irrelevant thoughts kept coming into her head. There was no focusing death, no keeping it steadily under the mind’s eye. In the end she found herself reading through what she had written putting in a stop here and there, correcting slips in the style, where it seemed to be too formal, too made-up, insufficiently spontaneous and unsuitable to the secret diary.

===Chinese===
她放下笔，透过敞开的窗户望着满天星斗，努力去想他，努力去想死亡。可是想死亡太难了。她发现，要让心念不间断地钉在寂灭上、钉在「无生而非生」上、钉在虚无上，实在太难。书里读到圣哲们如何冥想。她自己也常常试着冥想。可不知怎么，总不见成效。种种鸡毛蒜皮不相干的念头不断往脑子里钻。死亡没法聚焦，没法让它稳稳当当地待在心眼底下。末了她发觉自己在通读写下的东西，这里添个句点，那里改改笔病——哪里显得太板正、太做作、不够自然、配不上这本秘密日记，就改哪里。

===Original===
At the end of the last paragraph she added another “darling Jim,” and she repeated the words to herself, aloud, again and again. The exercise produced its usual effect; she felt the tears coming into her eyes.

The Quakers pray as the spirit moves them; but to let oneself be moved by the spirit is an arduous business. Kindlier and more worldly churches, with a feeling for human weakness, provide their worshippers with rituals, litanies, beads and prayer wheels.

“Darling Jim, darling Jim.” Miss Thriplow had found the form of words for her worship. “Darling Jim.” The tears did her good; she felt better, kinder, softer. And then, suddenly, she seemed to be listening to herself from outside. “Darling Jim.” But did she really care at all? Wasn’t it all a comedy, all a pretence? He had died so long ago; he had nothing to do with her now. Why should she care or remember? And all this systematic thinking about him, this writing of things in a secret diary devoted to his memory—wasn’t all that merely for the sake of keeping her emotions in training? Wasn’t she deliberately scratching her heart to make it bleed, and then writing stories with the red fluid?

===Chinese===
在最后一段的末尾她又添了一个“亲爱的吉姆”，还把这几个字对自己出声地念了一遍又一遍。这功课产生了惯常的功效；她觉得眼泪涌上了眼眶。

贵格会的教友随圣灵的感动祷告；可要让自己受圣灵感动，是桩苦差。更体贴人情、更入世的各派教会，体谅人的软弱，给信众备下了礼仪、连祷文、念珠和转经轮。

“亲爱的吉姆，亲爱的吉姆。”思里普洛小姐为她的敬拜找到了经文。“亲爱的吉姆。”眼泪对她有好处；她觉得好受了些，更善良了，更温柔了。接着，突然之间，她仿佛从外头听见了她自己。“亲爱的吉姆。”可她到底在乎不在乎呢？这一切难道不全是一出喜剧、一场做戏么？他死了那么久；如今同她已毫无干系。她凭什么要在乎、要记得？而这番对他的系统性思念，这本献给他亡灵的秘密日记里的种种文字——难道不全是为了让感情保持操练么？她岂不是在存心抓挠自己的心，叫它出血，然后拿那红色的液体写小说么？

===Original===
Miss Thriplow put away the thoughts as soon as they occurred to her: put them aside indignantly. They were monstrous thoughts, lying thoughts.

She picked up her pen again and wrote, very quickly, as though she were writing an exorcizing spell and the sooner it had been put on paper the sooner the evil thoughts would vanish.

“Do you remember, Jim, that time we went out in the canoe together and nearly got drowned?…”

===Chinese===
这些念头一冒出来，思里普洛小姐立刻把它们撵走：愤愤地撵到一边。它们是可怖的念头，撒谎的念头。

她重新拿起笔，飞快地写，仿佛在写一道驱邪的咒，越早落成白纸黑字，那些恶念就消失得越早。

“你可记得，吉姆，那一回我们一同划独木舟出去，差一点淹死？……”

## Part II Fragments from the Autobiography of Francis Chelifer / 第二部 弗朗西斯·切利弗自传断章

### I / I

===Original===
Old gentlemen in clubs were not more luxuriously cradled than I along the warm Tyrrhenian. Arms outstretched, like a live cross, I floated face upwards on that blue and tepid sea. The sun beat down on me, turning the drops on my face and chest to salt. My head was pillowed in the unruffled water; my limbs and body dimpled the surface of a pellucid mattress thirty feet thick and cherishingly resilient through all its thickness, down to the sandy bed on which it was spread. One might lie paralysed here for a lifetime and never get a bedsore.

===Chinese===
俱乐部里躺在软椅上的老绅士们，也未必比我当年在第勒尼安暖海里享受的摇篮更奢侈。两臂摊开，像一个活的十字架，我仰面漂浮在那片蓝色的温水海上。太阳直晒着我，把我脸上和胸口的晶莹水珠晒成盐粒。我的头枕在纹丝不乱的水里；我的四肢和身子，把一张三十英尺厚的透明褥垫的表面压出一个个小涡——那褥垫从表层直到铺展它的沙质海底，通体都那么柔韧、那么体贴地回弹着你。人可以在这里瘫痪着躺上一辈子，也决不会生褥疮。

===Original===
The sky above me was filmy with the noonday heat. The mountains, when I turned towards the land to look for them, had almost vanished behind a veil of gauze. But the Grand Hotel, on the other hand, though not perhaps quite so grand as it appeared in its illustrated prospectus—for there the front door was forty feet high and four tall acrobats standing on one another’s shoulders could not have reached to the sills of the ground-floor windows—the Grand Hotel made no attempt to conceal itself; the white villas glared out unashamedly from their groves of pines; and in front of them, along the tawny beach, I could see the bathing huts, the striped umbrellas, the digging children, the bathers splashing and wallowing in the hot shallows—half-naked men like statues of copper, girls in bright tunics, little boiled shrimps instead of little boys, and sleek ponderous walruses with red heads, who were the matrons in their rubber caps and their wet black bathing garments. Here and there over the surface of the sea moved what the natives called *patini*—catamarans made of a pair of boxed-in pontoons joined together near the ends and with a high seat for the rower in the middle. Slowly, trailing behind them as they went loud wafts of Italian gallantry, giggles and song, they crawled across the flat blueness. Sometimes, at the head of its white wake, its noise and its stink, a motor boat would pass, and suddenly my transparent mattress would rock beneath me, as the waves of its passage lifted me and let me drop and lifted me up again, more and ever more languidly, till all was once more smooth.

===Chinese===
头顶的天空被正午的暑气熏得蒙上一层薄膜。朝陆地那边转头去找群山时，山几乎已经隐没在一重纱幕后面。可大饭店却不然——尽管它未必像自家插图说明册里那样宏丽（在那上面，正门高达四十英尺，四个高个子杂技演员摞起肩来也够不着底楼的窗台）——大饭店无意掩藏自己；一幢幢白色别墅从松林里毫不害臊地瞪眼望出来；别墅前，沿着黄褐色的海滩，我望得见更衣棚、条纹阳伞、挖沙子的小孩，还有在滚热的浅水里扑腾打滚的浴客——半裸的男人像一尊尊铜像，穿鲜艳束腰外衣的姑娘们，小男孩活像一盘盘煮红的小虾，还有一头头油光水滑、臃肿笨重、红脑袋的海象——那是些戴橡胶浴帽、穿湿漉漉黑泳装的太太们。海面上到处移动着当地人称作 *patini*（帕蒂尼）的小船——一种双体筏，由两只封箱式的浮筒在近两端处并连而成，中间为桨手设一个高座。它们慢吞吞地爬过平坦的蓝面，身后拖着一股股响亮的意大利式献殷勤、咯咯的笑声和歌唱。偶尔，一艘汽艇顶着它的白色尾流、它的噪音和它的臭气驶过，我那张透明的褥垫便在我身下摇荡起来——它掀起的浪把我托起又放下，又托起，越托越懒、越托越懒，直到一切复归平滑。

===Original===
So much for that. The description, as I see now that I come to reread it, is not inelegant. For though I may not have played a hand of Bridge since I was eight and have never learned mah-jongg, I can claim at least to have studied the rules of style. I have learned the art of writing well, which is the art of saying nothing elaborately. I have acquired all the literary accomplishments. But then, if I may say so without fatuity, I also have a talent. “Nothing profits more than self-esteem founded on just and right.” I have Milton on my side to justify me in my assertion. When I write well, it is not merely another way of writing badly about nothing. In this respect my effusions differ a little from those of my cultured colleagues. I occasionally have something to say, and I find that the elegant but florid saying of it is as easy to me as walking. Not, of course, that I attach the slightest importance to that. I might have as much to say as La Rochefoucauld and as much facility for saying it as Shelley. But what of that? It would be great art, you say. No doubt; but what of *that*? It’s a queer prejudice, this one of ours in favour of art. Religion, patriotism, the moral order, humanitarianism, social reform—we have all of us, I imagine, dropped all those overboard long ago. But we still cling pathetically to art. Quite unreasonably; for the thing has far less reason for existence than most of the objects of worship we have got rid of, is utterly senseless, indeed, without their support and justification. Art for art’s sake—halma for halma’s sake. It is time to smash the last and silliest of the idols. My friends, I adjure you, put away the ultimate and sweetest of the inebriants and wake up at last completely sober—among the dustbins at the bottom of the area steps.

===Chinese===
到此为止。这段描写，如今回过头再读一遍，我得承认并不失雅致。我虽然从八岁起就没摸过一盘桥牌，也从未学会麻将，但文风的规矩至少是钻研过的。我学过把文章写好的艺术，那就是煞有介事地言之无物的艺术。种种文学的功夫我都齐全。而且，倘容我不带虚骄地说一句：我还有天分。「建立在公正与正义之上的自重，再没有比这更有益的了。」我说这话，背后有弥尔顿替我撑腰。我写得好，并不是把无物写得坏的又一种方式。在这一层上，我的舞文弄墨同我那些有教养的同行们的多少有点两样。我偶尔是有话要说的，而且我发现，把它说得华美而繁花似锦，对我来说易如走路。当然喽，我并不因此就把这事看得有一丝一毫的要紧。我尽可以像拉罗什富科那样有那么多话可说，像雪莱那样说得轻巧。可那又如何？您会说：那是伟大的艺术。无疑；可*那*又如何？这是我们的一种怪偏见——我们这种崇尚艺术的偏见。宗教、爱国主义、道德秩序、人道主义、社会改良——我想我们大家早已把那些统统抛下船去了。可我们还死抱着艺术不放，怪可怜见的。全然不讲道理；因为这东西存在的理由，远比我们扔掉的大多数崇拜对象更站不住脚；失去了它们的支持和论证，它实在是全无意义的。为艺术而艺术——为跳棋而跳棋。该是把最后一尊、也是最蠢的一尊偶像砸碎的时候了。朋友们，我恳求你们，撇下这最末、最甘美的迷魂酒吧，彻彻底底清醒过来吧——醒在地下室的台阶底下、垃圾桶中间。

===Original===
This little digression will suffice, I hope, to show that I labour, while writing, under no illusions. I do not suppose that anything I do has the slightest importance, and if I take so much pains in imparting beauty and elegance to these autobiographical fragments, it is chiefly from force of habit. I have practised the art of literature so long that it comes natural to me to take the pains I have always taken. You may ask why I write at all, if I regard the process as being without importance? It is a pertinent question. Why do you do this inconsistent thing? I can only plead weakness in justification. On principle I disapprove of writing; on principle I desire to live brutishly like any other ordinary human being. The flesh is willing, but the spirit is weak. I confess I grow bored. I pine for amusements other than those legitimate distractions offered by the cinema and the Palais de Danse. I struggle, I try to resist the temptation; but in the end I succumb. I read a page of Wittgenstein, I play a little Bach; I write a poem, a few aphorisms, a fable, a fragment of autobiography. I write with care, earnestly, with passion even, just as if there were some point in what I were doing, just as if it were important for the world to know my thoughts, just as if I had a soul to save by giving expression to them. But I am well aware, of course, that all these delightful hypotheses are inadmissible. In reality I write as I do merely to kill time and amuse a mind that is still, in spite of all my efforts, a prey to intellectual self-indulgence. I look forward to a placid middle age when, having finally overcome the old Adam in me, finally quenched all the extravagant spiritual cravings, I shall be able to settle down in tranquillity to that life of the flesh, that natural human existence which still, I fear, seems to me so forbidding, so austerely monotonous, so tedious. I have not yet attained to that blessed state. Hence these divagations into art; let me beg forgiveness for them. And above all, let me implore you once more not to imagine that I attach the slightest importance to them. My vanity would be hurt if I thought you did.

===Chinese===
这一小段离题话，我希望足以表明：我写作时并不存幻想。我不认为自己做的事情有丝毫要紧；我之所以肯下这么多功夫，给这些自传断章添上美和雅，主要是积习使然。文学这门艺术我操练得太久，不下惯常下的那些苦功，反而不自然了。您要问：既然认为这过程不要紧，那还写它做什么？问得在理。你为什么做这种自相矛盾的事？我只能拿软弱来申辩。原则上我不赞成写作；原则上我巴不得像任何一个普通人一样，畜生似的活着。肉体倒是愿意的，无奈心灵软弱了。我承认我会腻烦。电影院和舞蹈宫提供的那些正当解闷满足不了我，我渴望别的消遣。我挣扎，我抵拒诱惑；可末了还是降了。我读一页维特根斯坦，弹一点巴赫；我写一首诗、几条警句、一篇寓言、一段自传。我写得用心、认真，甚至带着激情，仿佛我做的事当真有什么名堂，仿佛世人当真该知道我的思想，仿佛我有个灵魂，一说出来就能得救。可我当然心里雪亮：这些可爱的假设统统不能成立。实际上，我之所以这样写，不过是为了消磨光阴，逗乐一颗心智——那心智任凭我怎样努力，仍旧摆脱不了智性上的自我放纵。我盼着一段安分的中年：到那时，我最终克除了身上的老亚当，最终扑灭了一切僭越的精神渴求，便能安然定居到肉体的生活里去，定居到那种自然的人生里去——虽然那人生我恐怕至今仍觉得面目可憎、严酷地单调、乏味得要命。我还没有修到那种有福的境界。于是有了这些往艺术里岔出去的漫游；请饶恕它们吧。而尤其是，让我再求您一回：千万别以为我把它们看得有一丝一毫的要紧。您若真那么想，我的虚荣心可要受伤了。

===Original===
Poor Mrs. Aldwinkle, for example—there was someone who could never believe that I was not an art-for-arter. “But Chelifer,” she used to say to me in her aimed, intent, breathless way, “how *can* you blaspheme like that against your own talent?” And I would put on my most Egyptian air—I have always been accused of looking like an Egyptian sculpture—my most Sphingine smile, and say: “But I am a democrat; how can I allow my talent to blaspheme against my humanity?”—or something enigmatic of that kind. Poor Mrs. Aldwinkle! But I run on too fast. I have begun to talk of Mrs. Aldwinkle and you do not know who Mrs. Aldwinkle is. Nor did I, for that matter, as I reclined that morning along the soft resilient water—I knew no more, then, than her name; who does not? Mrs. Aldwinkle the salonnière, the hostess, the giver of literary parties and agapae of lions—is she not classical? a household word? a familiar quotation? Of course. But in the flesh, till that moment, I had never seen her. Not through any lack of exertions on her part. For only a few months before, a telegram had arrived for me at my publisher’s: “**Prince Papadiamantopoulos just arrived most anxious to know best literary artistic intellectual society in London could you dine meet him Thursday eight fifteen 112 Berkeley Square Lilian Aldwinkle.**” In this telegraphic form, and couched in those terms, the invitation had certainly seemed alluring. But a little judicious inquiry showed me that the prospect was not really quite so attractive as it appeared. For Prince Papadiamantopoulos turned out, in spite of his wonderfully promising title and name, to be a perfectly serious intellectual like the rest of us. More serious indeed; for I discovered, to my horror, that he was a first-class geologist and could understand the differential calculus. Among the other guests were to be at least three decent writers and one painter. And Mrs. Aldwinkle herself was rumoured to be quite well educated and not entirely a fool. I filled up the reply-paid form and took it to the nearest post office. “**Much regret never dine out except in Lent Francis Chelifer.**” During Lent I confidently expected to receive another invitation. I was relieved, however, and a little disappointed, to hear no more from Mrs. Aldwinkle. I should have liked her to make, in vain, a further effort to lure me from my allegiance to Lady Giblet.

===Chinese===
可怜的奥尔德温克尔太太，比方说——就有这么一种人，怎么也没法相信我不是个为艺术而艺术的家伙。「可是切利弗，」她常用她那种有的放矢、全神贯注、气都喘不上来的口气对我说，「您*怎么*能这样渎犯自己的天分呢？」我便摆出我最埃及化的神气——我一向被人说长得像埃及雕像——我最斯芬克斯式的微笑，答道：「可我是个民主派；我怎么能容我的天分渎犯我的人性呢？」——或者诸如此类高深莫测的话。可怜的奥尔德温克尔太太！不过我扯得太快了。我说起奥尔德温克尔太太来了，可您还不知道奥尔德温克尔太太是谁呢。说实在的，那天早晨我舒舒服服躺在那片柔软弹性的水面上时，也不知道——那时我对她的了解，只限于她的名字；可谁不了解呢？沙龙女主人（salonnière）奥尔德温克尔太太，女主人，文学晚会和名流爱宴的操办者——她还不经典么？还不是家喻户晓？还不是一条熟典？当然。可肉身的她，直到那一刻为止，我从未见过。这倒不是她没下过力气。就在几个月前，我的出版商那里给我转到一封电报：「**帕帕迪亚曼托普洛斯亲王甫抵亟欲结识伦敦最佳文学艺术知识界可否周四八点一刻来晚餐一见伯克利广场 112 号莉莲·奥尔德温克尔**」这封电报体、如此措辞的邀约，看上去的确诱人。可略作审慎的打听便知道，前景实在不像表面那么动人。因为帕帕迪亚曼托普洛斯亲王——尽管名衔前程远大得吓人——竟同我们大家一样，是个十足正经的知识人。比正经还正经；因为我骇然发现，他是个第一流的地质学家，还看得懂微积分。其他宾客里至少有三位像样的作家、一位画家。而奥尔德温克尔太太本人，据传也颇有教养，并非全然的傻瓜。我填好回电费已付的电单，拿到最近的邮局去了。「**甚憾除大斋期外从不外出晚餐弗朗西斯·切利弗**」我满有把握地等着大斋期里再接到一封邀约。结果再没有奥尔德温克尔太太的消息，我松了口气，也有点失望。我本巴望她再白费一回力气，把我从对吉布利特夫人的效忠里勾引走。

===Original===
Ah, those evenings at Lady Giblet’s—I never miss a single one if I can help it. The vulgarity, ignorance and stupidity of the hostess, the incredible second-rateness of her mangy lions—these are surely unique. And then those camp-followers of the arts, those delicious Bohemians who regard their ability to appreciate the paintings of the cubists and the music of Stravinsky as a sufficient justification for helping themselves freely to one another’s wives—nowhere can you see such brilliant specimens of the type as at Lady Giblet’s. And the conversations one hears within those marble halls—nowhere, surely, are pretensions separated from justifying facts by a vaster gulf. Nowhere can you hear the ignorant, the illogical, the incapable of thought talking so glibly about things of which they have not the slightest understanding. And then you should hear them boasting parenthetically, as they express an imbecile’s incoherent opinion, of their own clear-headedness, their modern outlook, their ruthless scientific intelligence. Surely you can find nothing so perfect in its kind as at Lady Giblet’s—I at least know of nothing more complete. At Mrs. Aldwinkle’s one might very likely hear a serious conversation; never by any chance in the salon of *my* choice.

===Chinese===
啊，吉布利特夫人家那些晚会——只要躲得开，我一场也不落。女主人的庸俗、无知和愚蠢，她那群寒碜名流登峰造极的二流——真是举世无双。还有那些艺术的随营贩子，那些可爱的波希米亚人——他们把自己能欣赏立体派绘画和斯特拉文斯基音乐的本事，当作随意染指彼此妻子的充分理由——哪儿也见不到像吉布利特夫人家那样漂亮的品种标本。还有那几座大理石厅堂里听得到的谈话——毫无疑问，哪儿也不会把虚骄同替它撑腰的事实隔开一道更宽的鸿沟。哪儿也听不见无知的人、不合逻辑的人、不会思考的人，这样伶牙俐齿地大谈他们一窍不通的东西。还有，您真该听听他们一面发表白痴般语无伦次的高见，一面在插入语里自夸头脑清楚、观点摩登、有那种毫不留情的科学智力。这一类东西，您肯定找不到比吉布利特夫人家更完备的典型——至少我不知道还有更完备的。在奥尔德温克尔太太家，倒很可能听上一场严肃的谈话；在*我*中意的那间客厅里，那是万万没有的。

===Original===
But that morning in the blue Tyrrhenian was the last of my life to be passed beyond the pale of Mrs. Aldwinkle’s acquaintanceship; it was also as nearly as possible the first of my future life. Fate seemed that morning to be in doubt whether to extinguish me completely or merely to make me acquainted with Mrs. Aldwinkle. Fortunately, as I like to think, it chose the latter alternative. But I anticipate.

I first saw Mrs. Aldwinkle on this particular morning without knowing who she was. From where I was lying on my mattress of blue brine I noticed a heavily laden *patino* bearing slowly down upon me from the shore. Perched high on the rower’s bench a tall young man was toiling languidly at the oars. His back against the bench, his hairy legs stretched out along the prow of one of the pontoons, sat a thickset oldish man with a red face and short white hair. The bow of the other pontoon accommodated two women. The elder and larger of them sat in front, trailing her legs in the water; she was dressed in a kilted bathing costume of flame-coloured silk and her hair was tied up in a pink bandana handkerchief. Immediately behind her there squatted, her knees drawn up to her chin, a very youthful slender little creature in a black maillot. In one of her hands she held a green parasol with which she kept off the sunlight from her elder companion. Within the cylinder of greenish shadow the pink and flame-coloured lady, whom I afterwards learnt to be Mrs. Aldwinkle herself, looked like a Chinese lantern lighted in a conservatory; and when an accidental movement of the young girl’s umbrella allowed the sunlight for a moment to touch her face, one could imagine that the miracle of the raising of Lazarus was being performed before one’s eyes—for the green and corpse-like hue suddenly left the features, the colours of health, a little inflamed by the reflections from the bathing dress, seemed to rush back. The dead lived. But only for an instant; for the solicitous care of the young girl soon reversed the miracle. The sunshade swung back into position, the penumbra of the greenhouse enveloped the glowing lamp and the living face once more became ghastly, as though it belonged to someone who had lain for three days in the tomb.

===Chinese===
可第勒尼安蓝海里的那个早晨，是我此生活在奥尔德温克尔太太交往圈之外的最后一个早晨；它也差不多是我未来人生的第一天。那天早晨，命运似乎还在踌躇：是把我干脆熄灭呢，还是只叫我认识奥尔德温克尔太太。所幸——我喜欢这么想——它挑了后一条路。不过我话说在前头了。

我头一回见到奥尔德温克尔太太，就在这个特别的早晨，当时并不知道她是谁。从我那张蓝色咸水褥垫上，我望见一条载得沉甸甸的 *patino*（帕蒂诺）从岸边朝我慢慢压过来。高踞在桨座上，一个高个子青年有气无力地摇着桨。背靠着座板、毛茸茸的长腿顺着一只浮筒的艇首伸直了的，坐着一个矮壮的上了年纪的人，红脸膛，短短的白头发。另一只浮筒的艇首坐着两个女人。年长而高大些的那个坐在前头，两条腿拖在水里；她穿一身火焰色绸料的褶裥泳装，头发扎在一块粉红印花大手帕里。紧挨着她背后，蹲着一个非常年轻纤细的小人儿，双膝收到下巴底下，一身黑色紧身泳衣。她一只手擎着一柄绿阳伞，替年长的同伴挡着日头。在那圈淡绿阴影的圆筒里，那位粉红与火焰色的夫人——后来我才得知她正是奥尔德温克尔太太本人——像温室里点起的一盏中国灯笼；而当姑娘的伞偶然一动、让阳光有一瞬碰到了她的脸，你简直可以想象拉撒路复活的奇迹正在你眼前上演——因为那发绿的、尸身似的颜色蓦地离开了那张脸，健康的颜色、被泳衣的反光熏得微红一点的健康的颜色，仿佛一下子涌了回来。死人活了。可只是一瞬；因为姑娘体贴入微的照管很快又把奇迹倒了回去。阳伞荡回原位，温室的半影重新裹住那盏发亮的灯，那张活生生的脸重新变得死灰，仿佛属于一个在坟墓里躺了三天的人。

===Original===
At the stern, seen clearly only when the ponderous boat was already beginning to pass me, sat another young woman with a pale face and large dark eyes. A tendril of almost black hair escaped from under her bathing cap and fell, like a curling whisker, down her cheek. A handsome young man with a brown face and brown muscular arms and legs sprawled along the stern of the other pontoon, smoking a cigarette.

The voices that faintly came to me from the approaching boat sounded, somehow, more familiar than those I had heard from other *patini*. I became aware, all at once, that they were speaking English.

===Chinese===
船尾上还坐着另一个年轻女人，脸色苍白，一双黑色的大眼睛——等那只笨重的船已经开始从我旁边划过去时，我才看清她。一绺近乎黑色的头发从浴帽底下逃出来，像一根卷曲的颊须，垂在她腮上。一个英俊的青年，棕色的脸、棕色的结实胳膊和腿，四仰八叉摊在另一只浮筒的尾部，抽着一支烟。

从驶近的船上隐隐飘来的说话声，不知怎么，比我从别的 *patini* 上听见的更耳熟。我猛然醒悟过来：他们在说英语。

===Original===
“The clouds,” I heard the old red-faced gentleman saying (he had just turned round, in obedience to a gesture from the Chinese lantern in the conservatory, to look at the piled-up masses of vapour that hung like another fantastic range above the real mountains), “the clouds you so much admire are only made possible by the earth’s excrementitious dust hanging in the air. There are thousands of particles to every cubic centimetre. The water vapour condenses round them in droplets sufficiently large to be visible. Hence the clouds—marvellous and celestial shapes, but with a core of dust. What a symbol of human idealism!” The melodious voice grew louder and louder as the young man dipped and dipped his oars. “Earthy particles transfigured into heavenly forms. The heavenly forms are not self-existent, not absolute. Dust writes these vast characters across the sky.”

Preserve me, I thought. Did I come to Marina di Vezza to listen to this sort of thing?

===Chinese===
「云，」我听见那位红脸膛的老先生在说（他刚遵照温室中国灯笼的一个手势回过头去，仰望悬在真山上头、像另一道奇山异岭似的堆积的云团），「您如此倾倒的这些云，全靠大地那排泄般的尘埃悬浮在空中才成得了形。每个立方厘米里有几千颗微粒。水汽在它们周围凝成肉眼看得见的小水珠。于是有了云——奇妙的、天上的形状，内核却是一粒粒尘埃。人类理想主义的多好的象征！」少年一下一下地落桨，那悦耳的嗓音越来越响。「尘世的微粒变容为天上的形。天上的形并不自存，并不绝对。是尘埃在天空上写下这些大字。」

保佑我吧，我心里想。难道我到韦扎滨海来，就是听这个的？

===Original===
In a voice loud but indistinct, and strangely unmusical, the Chinese lantern lady began to quote Shelley, incorrectly. “‘From peak to peak in a bridge-like…’” she began, and relapsed into silence, clawing the air in search of the synonym for shape which ought to rhyme with peak. “‘Over a something sea.’ I think ‘The Cloud’ is almost the loveliest of all. It’s wonderful to think that Shelley sailed in this sea. And that he was burnt only a little way off, down there.” She pointed down the coast to where, behind the haze, the interminable seafront of Viareggio stretched away mile after mile. Faintly now one might discern the ghost of its nearest outskirts. But at evening it would emerge; clear and sharp in the sloping light, as though they had been cut from gems, Palace and Grande Bretagne, Europe (*già* Aquila Nera) and Savoia would twinkle there, majestic toys, among the innumerable lesser inns and boardinghouses, reduced at this distance to an exquisite loveliness and so pathetically small and delicate that one could almost have wept over them. At this very moment, on the other side of the curtain of haze, a hundred thousand bathers were thronging the empty beaches where Shelley’s body had been committed to the fire. The pinewoods in which, riding out from Pisa, he hunted lovely thoughts through the silence and the fragrant shadows teemed now with life. Unnumbered country copulatives roamed at this moment through those glades.… And so forth. Style pours out of my fountain pen. In every drachm of blue-black ink a thousand *mots justes* are implicit, like the future characteristics of a man in a piece of chromosome. I apologize.

===Chinese===
那位中国灯笼夫人开始引用雪莱，用一副响亮而不清晰、又出奇地不成腔调的嗓音，而且引错了。「『从峰巅到峰巅，像一座桥似的……』」她起了个头，便归于沉默，在空中抓挠着，搜寻那个该同「峰巅」押韵的、「形状」的同义词。「『在一片什么的海上。』我想《云》差不多是所有诗里顶可爱的。想想看，雪莱在这片海上驶过帆船。还有，他就在那边不远、下首那儿被烧掉的。」她朝海岸下首指去——在霾翳后面，维亚雷焦那望不到头的滨海长街正在那里一英里接一英里地延伸。此刻你还能隐约辨出它近郊的鬼影。可到了傍晚它就会浮现出来；在斜光里又清晰又锐利，宫殿饭店、大不列颠饭店、欧罗巴饭店（*già*，旧称黑鹰饭店）和萨伏伊饭店，像从宝石上切下来似的，在数不清的较小客栈和公寓旅馆中间熠熠闪亮，成了庄严的玩具；隔着这段距离，它们缩成一种精美入骨的可爱，小得、巧得叫人几乎要为它们落泪。而就在此刻，在霾幕的那一边，十万浴客正挤满空空的沙滩——雪莱的遗体就是在这沙滩上火化的。他从比萨骑马出来、在静谧和芬芳的浓荫里猎取可爱念头的那些松林，如今人满为患。此刻正有数不清的乡下配偶在那片林间空地上游荡。……诸如此类。文风从我的自来水笔里滔滔涌出。每一打兰蓝黑墨水里都蕴含着一千个 *mots justes*（恰到好处的字眼），就像一段染色体里蕴含着一个人的全部未来品性。我道歉。

===Original===
Youth, then, at the prow and pleasure at the helm—and the flesh was so glossy under the noonday sun, the colours so blazingly bright, that I was really reminded of Etty’s little ravishment—the laden boat passed slowly within a few yards of me. Stretched like a live cross on my mattress of brine I looked at them languidly through half-closed eyes. They looked at me; a blank incuriosity was on their faces—for a glimpse only, then they averted their eyes as though I had been one of those exhausted frogs one sees, after the breeding season, floating belly upwards on the surface of a pond. And yet I was what is technically known as an immortal soul. It struck me that it would have been more reasonable if they had stopped their boat and hailed me across the water. “Good morning, stranger. How goes your soul? And what shall we do to be saved?” But on the other hand, our habit of regarding strangers as being nothing more to us than exhausted frogs probably saves a good deal of trouble.

“From cape to cape,” emended the red-faced gentleman, as they receded from me.

And very diffidently, in a soft shy voice, the solicitous young creature suggested that the something sea was a torrent sea.

“Whatever vat may be,” said the young rower, whose exertions under the broiling sun entitled him to take the professionally nautical, commonsense view about the matter.

“But it’s obvious what it is,” said the Chinese lantern lady, rather contemptuously. The young man at the stern threw away his cigarette and started meditatively whistling the tune of “*Deh, vieni alla finestra*” from *Don Giovanni*.

===Chinese===
于是，青春在船首，快乐在舵柄——正午烈日下的皮肉那么光亮，颜色那么灼灼逼人，我当真被提起了埃蒂那幅小小的劫夺图——那条载得沉甸甸的船在离我几码的地方缓缓驶过。我像个活的十字架摊在我的咸水褥垫上，眯缝着眼，懒洋洋地望着他们。他们也望着我；脸上是一片空茫的无好奇——只一瞥，随后就把眼睛挪开，仿佛我是繁殖季节过后、肚皮朝天漂在池面上的那些精疲力竭的青蛙当中的一只。而我却是术语上所谓的不朽灵魂。我忽然想到：他们本该把船停下，隔水向我招呼一声，那才更合理些。「早上好，异乡人。您的灵魂可好？我们该怎样才可以得救？」可话又说回来，我们习惯于把陌生人看成不比一只脱力的青蛙更了不起的东西，这习惯恐怕省去了不少麻烦。

「从海角到海角，」红脸膛先生在他们离我远去时纠正道。

那个体贴的年轻小人儿又怯生生地、用柔软腼腆的嗓音提出来：那片「什么的海」，是激流的海。

「管它素什么意思吧，」年轻的桨手说——他在毒日头底下出的这身汗，给了他就此事发表职业水手式常识见解的资格。

「可它明摆着是什么呀，」中国灯笼夫人相当轻蔑地说。船尾那个青年把烟头一丢，若有所思地吹起口哨来，吹的是《唐·乔万尼》里那支「*Deh, vieni alla finestra*」（嗐，到窗前来吧）。

===Original===
There was a silence; the boat receded, stroke after stroke. The last words I heard were uttered, drawlingly and in a rather childish voice, by the young woman in the stern. “I wish I could get brown more quickly,” she said, lifting one foot out of the water and looking at the white bare leg. “One might have been living in a cellar. Such a dreadfully unwholesome look of blanched asparagus. Or even mushrooms,” she added pensively.

The Chinese lantern lady said something, then the red-faced man. But the conversation had ceased to be articulately audible. Soon I could hear no more; they had gone, leaving behind them, however, the name of Shelley. It was here, along these waters, that he had sailed his flimsy boat. In one hand he held his Sophocles, with the other the tiller. His eyes looked now at the small Greek letters, now to the horizon, or landwards towards the mountains and clouds. “Port your helm, Shelley,” Captain Williams would shout. And the helm went hard over to starboard; the ship staggered, almost capsized. Then, one day, flash! the black opaque sky split right across; crash and rumble! the thunder exploded overhead and with the noise of boulders being trundled over the surface of the metal clouds, the echoes rolled about the heavens and among the mountains—“from peak to peak,” it occurred to me, adopting the Chinese lantern lady’s emendation, “from peak to peak with a gong-like squeak.” (What an infamy!) And then, with a hiss and a roar, the whirl-blast was upon them. It was all over.

===Chinese===
一阵沉默；小船一桨一桨地远去。我听见的最后几句话，是船尾那位年轻女子拖着长腔、用相当孩子气的嗓音说的。「但愿我晒黑得快些，」她说着，把一只脚从水里提起来，端详那条白生生的光腿。「人家还当我在地窖里过日子呢。好一股漂白芦笋似的病态的白。干脆说蘑菇得了，」她若有所思地补了一句。

中国灯笼夫人说了句什么，随后是红脸膛的男人。可谈话已经听不真切了。不多时我就什么也听不见了；他们走了，然而把雪莱的名字留在了身后。就在这里，沿着这片海水，他驾着他那艘单薄的小船。一只手擎着他的索福克勒斯，另一只手把着舵柄。眼睛一会儿看那些小小的希腊字母，一会儿望海平线，或者朝陆地望群山和云。「把你的舵打向左舷，雪莱，」威廉斯船长会这样喊。于是舵猛地打向右舷；船一个趔趄，几乎翻了。后来有一天，唰！黑沉沉不透光的天空当空裂开；轰隆、炸响！雷霆在头顶爆开，伴着巨石在金属的云面上滚过的声音，回声在天上和群山之间滚来滚去——「从峰巅到峰巅，」我忽然想起，顺手采纳了中国灯笼夫人的改法，「从峰巅到峰巅，带着锣似的尖叫。」（何等的不公！）然后，一声嘶吼，旋风劈头盖脸扑到了他们身上。一切就此完了。

===Original===
Even without the Chinese lantern lady’s hint I should probably have started thinking of Shelley. For to live on this coast, between the sea and the mountains, among alternate flawless calms and shattering sudden storms, is like living inside one of Shelley’s poems. One walks through a transparent and phantasmagorical beauty. But for the hundred thousand bathers, the jazz band in the Grand Hotel, the unbroken front which civilization, in the form of boardinghouses, presents for miles at a time to the alien and empty sea, but for all these, one might seriously lose one’s sense of reality and imagine that fancy had managed to transform itself into fact. In Shelley’s days, when the coast was all but uninhabited, a man might have had some excuse for forgetting the real nature of things. Living here in an actual world practically indistinguishable from one of imagination, a man might almost be justified for indulging his fancy to the extravagant lengths to which Shelley permitted his to go.

But a man of the present generation, brought up in typical contemporary surroundings, has no justifications of this sort. A modern poet cannot permit himself the mental luxuries in which his predecessors so freely wallowed. Lying there on the water, I repeated to myself some verses, inspired by reflections like these, which I had written some few months before.

===Chinese===
即便没有中国灯笼夫人那一提，我大概也会想起雪莱。因为生活在这段海岸上，夹在海与山之间，穿插着无瑕的平静和天崩地裂的骤雨，就像生活在一首雪莱的诗的里面。人穿行在一种透明的、幻影重重的美里。若不是那十万浴客、大饭店里的爵士乐队、那道以公寓旅馆的形态一连几英里面对着陌生而空旷的大海的文明阵线，若不是这一切，人真要丢掉现实感，当真以为幻想居然化成了事实。在雪莱的时代，这段海岸几乎无人居住，一个人忘记事物的真实本性还情有可原。住在这样一个同想象世界几乎无从分辨的现实世界里，一个人纵容自己的幻想、纵容到雪莱容许他自己达到的那等放肆地步，也几乎算得情有可原。

可是当今一代、在典型的现代环境里长大的人，就没有这类情有可原了。一个现代诗人再不能容许自己沉溺于前辈们随意打滚的那些精神奢侈。躺在水面上，我向自己背诵了几行诗——那是几个月前写的，灵感正来自诸如此类的感想。

===Original===
> The Holy Ghost comes sliding down
 
 
 On Ilford, Golders Green and Penge.
 
 
 His hosts infect him as they rot;
 
 
 The victims take their just revenge.
> For if of old the sons of squires
 
 
 And livery stable keepers turned
 
 
 To flowers and hope, to Greece and God,
 
 
 We in our later age have learned
> That we are native where we walk
 
 
 Through the dim streets of Camden Town.
 
 
 But hopeful still through twice-breathed air
 
 
 The Holy Ghost comes shining down.

===Chinese===
> 圣灵滑翔着降临，
> 降在伊尔福德、戈尔德斯格林和佩奇。
> 宿主们腐烂着把他感染；
> 受害者们讨还他们应得的报偿。
> 昔日，乡绅的儿子们
> 和车马行的掌柜们
> 曾经转向花卉与希望、希腊与上帝；
> 而我们这晚近的时代已经懂得：
> 我们本乡本土，就在我们
> 走过其间的卡姆登镇昏暗街道上。
> 但圣灵仍怀着希望，穿过二度呼吸的空气，
> 熠熠生辉地降临。

===Original===
I wrote these lines, I remember, one dark afternoon in my office in Gog’s Court, Fetter Lane. It is in the same office, on an almost perfectly similar afternoon, that I am writing now. The reflector outside my window reflects a faint and muddy light that has to be supplemented by electricity from within. An inveterate smell of printer’s ink haunts the air. From the basement comes up the thudding and clanking of presses; they are turning out the weekly two hundred thousand copies of the *Woman’s Fiction Budget*. We are at the heart, here, of our human universe. Come, then, let us frankly admit that we are citizens of this mean city, make the worst of it resolutely and not try to escape.

===Chinese===
我记得，这几行诗是某个昏暗的下午，在芬特尔巷戈格大院我的办公室里写的。此刻我执笔的，也正是这间办公室，也是一个几乎一模一样的下午。窗外那面反光板反进一线昏黄浑浊的光，还非得靠屋里的电灯来补足。一股积年不散的油墨味在空气里游荡。地下室里传上来印刷机的咚咚哐哐；他们正印着每周二十万份的《女性小说汇编》。我们此刻正坐在人类宇宙的心脏上。来吧，让我们坦白承认自己是这座鄙陋之城的市民，认命地往最坏处想，别再想逃。

===Original===
To escape, whether in space or in time, you must run a great deal further now than there was any need to do a hundred years ago when Shelley boated on the Tyrrhenian and conjured up millennial visions. You must go further in space, because there are more people, more and faster vehicles. The Grand Hotel, the hundred thousand bathers, the jazz bands have introduced themselves into that Shelleian poem which is the landscape of Versilia. And the millennium which seemed in the days of Godwin not so very remote has receded further and further from us, as each Reform Bill, each victory over entrenched capitalism dashed yet another illusion to the ground. To escape, in 1924, one must go to Tibet, one must look forward to at least the year 3000; and who knows? they are probably listening-in in the Dalai Lama’s palace; and it is probable that the millennial state of a thousand years hence will be millennial only because it has contrived to make slavery, for the first time, really scientific and efficient.

===Chinese===
要逃——无论在空间里还是时间里——如今你都得跑得比一百年前远得多；那时节雪莱在第勒尼安海上荡舟，召唤千年幻象，还用不着跑多远。在空间上你得更远，因为人更多、车也更多更快。大饭店、十万浴客、爵士乐队，已经自己挤进了韦尔西利亚风景这首雪莱的诗。而那在戈德文的时代显得并不遥远的千年盛世，随着一项项改革法案、一次次对盘踞的资本主义的胜利把又一个幻梦摔碎在地，离我们越来越远。到了 1924 年，要逃就得去西藏，就得指望至少公元 3000 年；可谁知道呢？达赖喇嘛的宫殿里恐怕也在听广播；而千年之后的那個千年盛世，说不定之所以千年，只是因为它头一回把奴役办得真正科学、真正高效。

===Original===
An escape in space, even if one contrives successfully to make one, is no real escape at all. A man may live in Tibet or among the Andes; but he cannot therefore deny that London and Paris actually exist, he cannot forget that there are such places as New York and Berlin. For the majority of contemporary human beings, London and Manchester are the rule; you may have fled to the eternal spring of Arequipa, but you are not living in what is, for the mass of human consciousness, reality.

===Chinese===
空间里的逃遁，就算你当真办成了，也根本算不得真逃。人可以住在西藏或安第斯山里；可他不能因此就否认伦敦和巴黎当真存在，不能忘记世上还有纽约和柏林这些地方。对当代人类的大多数来说，伦敦和曼彻斯特才是常规；你可以逃到阿雷基帕的永恒春天里去，可你并不生活在大众意识所认定的那个现实里。

===Original===
An escape in time is no more satisfactory. You live in the radiant future, live for the future. You console yourself for the spectacle of things as they are by the thought of what they will be. And you work, perhaps, to make them be what you think they ought to be. I know all about it, I assure you. I have done it all myself—lived in a state of permanent intoxication at the thought of what was to come, working happily for a gorgeous ideal of happiness. But a little reflection suffices to show how absurd these forward lookings, these labours for the sake of what is to be, really are. For, to begin with, we have no reason to suppose that there is going to be a future at all, at any rate for human beings. In the second place we do not know whether the ideal of happiness towards which we are striving may not turn out either to be totally unrealizable or, if realizable, utterly repellent to humanity. Do people want to be happy? If there were a real prospect of achieving a permanent and unvarying happiness, wouldn’t they shrink in horror from the boring consummation? And finally, the contemplation of the future, the busy working for it, does not prevent the present from existing. It merely partially blinds us to the present.

The same objections apply with equal force to the escapes which do not launch out into space or time, but into Platonic eternity, into the ideal. An escape into mere fancy does not prevent facts from going on; it is a disregarding of the facts.

===Chinese===
时间里的逃遁也强不到哪儿去。你住在光辉的未来里，为未来而活。你拿「事物将会怎样」的念头，来宽解「事物现在怎样」的景象。也许你还干活，好叫它们变成你以为它们该变成的样子。这一套我全懂，我向您保证。我自己全都干过——一想到将来就处在一种持久的陶醉里，为一个辉煌的幸福理想而快活地工作。可略一反思便足以看出，这些前瞻、这些为「将然」下的苦力，实在荒唐。因为，第一，我们没有理由假定将来到底有没有，至少对人类而言。第二，我们并不知道，我们奋力趋赴的那个幸福理想，到头来会不会全然实现不了，或者实现了，却全然叫人类生厌。人们想要幸福么？倘若真有一线希望达成一种恒定不变的幸福，他们岂不要在那场乏味的圆满跟前骇然退缩？最后，对未来沉思、为未来奔忙，并不能叫现在不存在。它只是把我们的眼睛对现在蒙去一半。

这些驳难，同样有力地适用于那些不往空间或时间里、而往柏拉图式永恒里、往理想里发射的逃遁。往纯粹幻想里的逃遁拦不住事实照旧进行；那是对事实的置之不理。

===Original===
Finally there are those people, more courageous than the escapers, who actually plunge into the real contemporary life around them, and are consoled by finding in the midst of its squalor, its repulsiveness and stupidity, evidences of a widespread kindliness, of charity, pity and the like. True, these qualities exist and the spectacle of them is decidedly cheering; in spite of civilization, men have not fallen below the brutes. Parents, even in human society, are devoted to their offspring; even in human society the weak and the afflicted are sometimes assisted.
